—

The Foundational Weirdness: Core Principles
These are the building blocks — the ideas that launched a scientific revolution and forced humanity to rethink what “reality” even means.

1. Wave-Particle Duality
Every quantum object — an electron, a photon of light, even a small molecule — behaves like both a wave and a particle, depending on how you look at it. This isn’t a matter of our ignorance; it’s a fundamental feature of nature.

The definitive proof came from the double-slit experiment. Fire electrons one at a time through two narrow slits in a barrier, and over time they create an interference pattern on the screen behind — the signature of waves overlapping. But the moment you try to detect which slit each electron went through, the interference pattern vanishes. The electrons behave like particles again. What they “are” depends on how you observe them, a fact that baffled Einstein and continues to challenge our deepest intuitions about physical objects.

zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
2. Quantum Superposition
In classical physics, a coin is either heads or tails. In quantum physics, until you look, it can be both at once. This is superposition: a quantum system exists in a combination of multiple states simultaneously until a measurement forces it into one definite outcome.

Erwin Schrödinger illustrated the absurdity with his famous 1935 thought experiment involving a cat sealed in a box with a radioactive atom. If quantum rules applied at the macroscopic scale, the cat would be simultaneously alive and dead until the box was opened. Schrödinger intended this as a reductio ad absurdum, but the core principle is real and experimentally verified for subatomic particles. Modern quantum computers exploit superposition directly — their quantum bits (qubits) can represent 0 and 1 simultaneously, enabling computations of staggering complexity.

3. Quantum Entanglement
Einstein called it “spooky action at a distance,” and he meant it as a criticism. When two particles become entangled, their quantum states are correlated in a way that cannot be explained by any pre-existing shared information. Measure one particle and instantly — regardless of whether its partner is across the room or across the galaxy — the other particle’s corresponding property is determined.

Introduction To Topology
Introduction To Topology
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
This doesn’t allow faster-than-light communication (the measurement outcomes are random), but it confirms that entangled particles share a non-local connection that defies our classical picture of separate, independent objects. Entanglement was experimentally confirmed with increasing rigor from the 1970s onwards and is now the backbone of quantum cryptography and quantum teleportation experiments.

4. The Observer Effect and the Measurement Problem
One of the most philosophically charged concepts in all of physics: the act of measuring a quantum system changes it. A quantum particle exists as a “wavefunction” — a mathematical description of all its possible states and probabilities. The moment it’s measured, this wavefunction collapses into a single definite value.

But what counts as an “observation”? Does it require a conscious observer? A detector? Simply an interaction with the environment? This is called the measurement problem, and physicists have argued about it for a century without consensus. What’s clear is that the boundary between the quantum world and the classical world we experience is not a sharp line — and figuring out exactly where it lies is one of the deepest unsolved problems in physics.

5. Heisenberg’s Uncertainty Principle
Werner Heisenberg demonstrated in 1927 that you can never simultaneously know both the precise position and the precise momentum of a quantum particle. The more accurately you pin down one, the more uncertain the other becomes. This is encoded in the formula ΔxΔp ≥ ħ/2, where ħ is the reduced Planck constant.

This isn’t a limitation of our instruments — it’s baked into the fabric of reality. The uncertainty arises because measuring a particle’s position requires hitting it with light, and that interaction disturbs its momentum. More fundamentally, the particle simply doesn’t have both a precise position and a precise momentum at the same time. The universe keeps certain pairs of facts mutually exclusive.

6. Quantum Tunneling
Classically, if a ball doesn’t have enough energy to roll over a hill, it stays on one side. In the quantum world, particles can pass through energy barriers they classically shouldn’t be able to cross — a phenomenon called tunneling. The particle’s wavefunction has a non-zero probability of existing on the other side of the barrier, and sometimes, it materializes there.

Quantum tunneling isn’t just theoretical — it’s essential for the universe as we know it. It enables nuclear fusion in the sun (protons tunnel through the electromagnetic repulsion barrier), makes radioactive alpha decay possible, and is the operating principle behind the scanning tunneling microscope, which can image individual atoms. The flash memory in your phone also relies on electrons tunneling through thin insulating layers.

7. Quantization of Energy
Max Planck ignited the quantum revolution in 1900 when he proposed that energy is not continuous — it comes in discrete packets called quanta. The energy of each quantum is given by E = hf, where h is Planck’s constant and f is the frequency of the radiation. Einstein later used this idea to explain the photoelectric effect (earning him the 1921 Nobel Prize), establishing that light itself travels in quantized packets called photons.

Quantization explains why atoms don’t collapse (electrons can only occupy specific energy levels, not spiral inward continuously), why heated objects glow specific colors, and why chemistry works at all. It’s the foundation on which everything else in quantum mechanics is built.

—

The Fabric of Reality: Fields, Particles, and Forces
Dig deeper, and the quantum world reveals itself as far stranger than individual particles bouncing around. Reality, at its most fundamental level, looks like something else entirely.

8. Quantum Field Theory (QFT)
Particles aren’t the fundamental objects of nature — fields are. Quantum Field Theory describes the universe as a collection of quantum fields — the electron field, the photon field, the quark field — that permeate all of space and time. What we call particles are simply localized excitations (ripples) in these fields, like bubbles rising in a quantum ocean.

QFT is the most precisely tested framework in the history of science. Its predictions for quantities like the electron’s magnetic moment match experimental measurements to 12 decimal places — a level of accuracy comparable to measuring the distance from New York to Los Angeles to within the width of a human hair.

9. Virtual Particles
According to QFT, “empty” space is teeming with activity. The uncertainty principle applies to energy and time as well as position and momentum, which means that for very short intervals, pairs of particles can spontaneously pop into existence and annihilate each other. These are called virtual particles, and while they can’t be directly detected, their effects are measurable.

The Casimir effect offers striking proof: place two uncharged metal plates nanometers apart in a vacuum, and they will be pushed together by a tiny but measurable force. The reason? Fewer virtual particle configurations fit between the plates than outside them, creating a net inward pressure. Virtual particles also mediate fundamental forces — the electromagnetic force between charged particles is transmitted by the exchange of virtual photons.

10. Quantum Electrodynamics (QED)
QED is the quantum theory of how light and matter interact. It describes every electromagnetic phenomenon — from the way magnets attract to why chemicals bond — in terms of electrons exchanging virtual photons. Developed principally by Richard Feynman, Julian Schwinger, and Sin-Itiro Tomonaga in the late 1940s, QED earned all three the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1965.

Feynman’s contribution included a brilliant visual shorthand — Feynman diagrams — which represent particle interactions as simple line drawings and allow physicists to calculate the probability of any electromagnetic event with extraordinary precision.

11. Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD)
If QED governs the electromagnetic force, QCD governs the strong nuclear force — the force that binds quarks together into protons and neutrons, and holds atomic nuclei together. Quarks carry a property called “color charge” (not actual color — just a label for three types of strong charge: red, green, and blue). Gluons are the force-carrying particles, analogous to photons in QED.

One of QCD’s strangest features is confinement: quarks are never found alone in nature. The more you try to pull two quarks apart, the stronger the force between them becomes — like stretching a rubber band until it snaps and creates two new quarks instead. This is why free quarks have never been directly observed.

12. The Standard Model
The Standard Model of particle physics is the master framework that combines QED, QCD, and the electroweak theory into a single coherent description of all known fundamental particles and three of the four fundamental forces. It lists 17 fundamental particles: 6 quarks, 6 leptons (including the electron and neutrinos), 4 force-carrying bosons (photon, W and Z bosons, gluon), and the Higgs boson — confirmed at CERN’s Large Hadron Collider in 2012.

Despite its success, the Standard Model is known to be incomplete. It doesn’t incorporate gravity, doesn’t explain dark matter or dark energy, and can’t account for the matter-antimatter asymmetry in the universe. It’s the best map we have, but large portions of the territory remain uncharted.

13. Zero-Point Energy
Even at absolute zero temperature — the coldest anything can theoretically get — quantum systems retain energy. This zero-point energy is a direct consequence of the uncertainty principle: a particle at rest would have perfectly defined position and momentum simultaneously, violating Heisenberg’s constraint. So particles perpetually jitter, even in their lowest energy state.

This isn’t just theoretical curiosity. Zero-point energy contributes to the stability of matter, drives the Casimir effect, and plays a role in superconductivity and superfluidity. It also tantalizes physicists and engineers with questions about whether this “ground state” energy of the vacuum could ever be harnessed — though current physics offers no practical mechanism for doing so.

14. Pauli Exclusion Principle
Proposed by Wolfgang Pauli in 1925, this principle states that no two identical fermions (particles with half-integer spin, like electrons, protons, and neutrons) can occupy the same quantum state at the same time. Two electrons in an atom can share an orbital only if they have opposite spins — and that’s their limit.

This principle is responsible for the structure of the periodic table, the stability of matter, and the existence of solid objects (which resist compression because electrons refuse to be squeezed into the same quantum state). It’s also what prevents neutron stars from collapsing: neutrons packed at extreme densities resist further compression because no two can share the same state, creating what physicists call neutron degeneracy pressure.

—

Challenging Our Intuition: Interpretations and Paradoxes
Quantum mechanics gives correct predictions. But what it means about the nature of reality is a question physicists and philosophers have argued about for a century — without resolution.

15. The Copenhagen Interpretation
The Copenhagen Interpretation, developed primarily by Niels Bohr and Werner Heisenberg in the late 1920s, is the closest thing quantum mechanics has to an official doctrine. Its core claim: a quantum system doesn’t have definite properties until it’s measured. The wavefunction is a complete description of what we can know, and asking what the particle was doing before measurement is meaningless.

Copenhagen became the pragmatic workhorse of quantum physics — “shut up and calculate” is its unofficial motto. It works brilliantly for predictions but dodges the deeper questions. What causes wavefunction collapse? What counts as a measurement? For many physicists, these silences are features, not bugs. For others, they’re a philosophical scandal.

16. The Many-Worlds Interpretation (MWI)
Proposed by Hugh Everett III in 1957, the Many-Worlds Interpretation offers a radical alternative to Copenhagen: the wavefunction never collapses. Instead, every quantum event causes the universe to branch into multiple copies, one for each possible outcome. In one branch, Schrödinger’s cat is alive. In another, it’s dead. Both exist — in parallel, non-communicating universes.

MWI eliminates the awkward collapse postulate and treats the universe as a single, giant, deterministic quantum system. It comes at a cost: an literally infinite proliferation of parallel universes. This makes many physicists uncomfortable on grounds of ontological excess. Nevertheless, MWI has gained significant traction, particularly among quantum computing researchers who find it mathematically elegant.

17. De Broglie-Bohm Theory (Pilot-Wave Theory)
What if particles do have definite positions and momenta at all times — we just can’t measure both simultaneously? The De Broglie-Bohm theory, also called pilot-wave theory, proposes exactly this. Particles are real, localized objects guided by a “pilot wave” that evolves according to the Schrödinger equation and tells them where to go.

This interpretation is deterministic and reproduces all the predictions of standard quantum mechanics. The weirdness is relocated to the pilot wave itself, which is inherently non-local — it spans the entire universe. Critics argue that pilot-wave theory is more complicated than Copenhagen without adding explanatory power. Proponents argue it’s the only interpretation that takes seriously the idea that particles are real things.

18. Bell’s Theorem
In 1964, physicist John Bell proved something remarkable: if the correlations between entangled particles are the result of hidden local variables (information shared at the moment the particles were created), then those correlations must satisfy certain statistical limits, encoded in Bell inequalities. Quantum mechanics predicts violations of these inequalities.

Experiments beginning with Alain Aspect’s landmark 1982 tests, and culminating in loophole-free tests in 2015, have conclusively shown that Bell inequalities are violated — exactly as quantum mechanics predicts. This means Einstein’s preferred alternative — that quantum weirdness is just ignorance of hidden local variables — is ruled out by experiment. The universe is genuinely, irreducibly non-local.

19. The EPR Paradox
In 1935, Einstein, Boris Podolsky, and Nathan Rosen published a thought experiment (the EPR paradox) designed to show that quantum mechanics was incomplete. They argued that if two particles are entangled and measuring one instantly affects the other, then either information travels faster than light (violating relativity), or the particle’s properties were determined all along by hidden variables that quantum mechanics ignores.

Einstein concluded that quantum mechanics couldn’t be the final word. He was wrong — Bell’s theorem and subsequent experiments showed that no hidden variable theory can replicate quantum predictions while remaining local. The EPR paradox is one of the most fertile failures in scientific history: it was wrong, but it pushed physics to confront the deepest questions about locality, reality, and the limits of knowledge.

20. The Quantum Zeno Effect
Named after the ancient Greek philosopher famous for paradoxes of motion, the Quantum Zeno Effect states that continuously observing an unstable quantum system can prevent it from evolving or decaying. Measure a radioactive atom frequently enough, and you can freeze it in its undecayed state — because each measurement resets its quantum clock.

This effect has been experimentally confirmed and isn’t merely philosophical. It has practical applications in quantum computing (stabilizing qubits against decoherence) and has been observed in systems ranging from cold atoms to photons. The inverse — the Quantum Anti-Zeno Effect, where frequent measurement accelerates decay — has also been confirmed, adding another layer of counterintuitive complexity.

21. The Delayed Choice Quantum Eraser
This is where quantum physics gets almost incomprehensibly strange. In a 1999 experiment by Yoon-Ho Kim and colleagues, the experimenters arranged a double-slit setup so that information about which path a photon took could be either preserved or destroyed after the photon had already hit the detector. When “which path” information was erased, an interference pattern appeared retroactively — even though the photon had already been detected.

This seems to suggest that future choices can influence past events. The more careful interpretation is that the results reflect quantum correlations that exist regardless of when we choose to reveal them — but the implications for causality and time are deeply unsettling. The delayed-choice quantum eraser remains one of the most-debated and mind-bending experiments in the history of physics.

—

Emerging Frontiers: Technology and Beyond
The quantum revolution isn’t just philosophical. It’s now producing technologies that will reshape civilization — and raising new questions at the edges of biology and cosmology.

22. Quantum Computing
Classical computers process information as bits — 0s and 1s. Quantum computers use qubits, which can be in superpositions of 0 and 1 simultaneously, and can be entangled with each other. This allows quantum computers to explore vast numbers of computational possibilities in parallel.

For certain problems — breaking current encryption schemes, simulating molecular dynamics for drug discovery, optimizing logistics networks — quantum computers promise exponential speedups over classical machines. Google claimed “quantum supremacy” in 2019, completing a specific calculation in 200 seconds that they estimated would take a classical supercomputer 10,000 years. The technology is still nascent and error-prone, but it represents one of the most transformative technological frontiers of the 21st century.

23. Quantum Cryptography
Quantum mechanics makes it physically impossible to eavesdrop on certain types of communication without being detected. In quantum key distribution (QKD), encryption keys are transmitted as quantum states (typically polarized photons). Any interception attempt disturbs these states in a detectable way — the laws of physics themselves become the security guarantee.

The first quantum key distribution protocol, BB84, was proposed by Charles Bennett and Gilles Brassard in 1984. China has already deployed the world’s largest quantum communication network, spanning over 4,600 kilometers, using a combination of ground-based fiber and quantum satellite links. As quantum computers threaten to break classical encryption, quantum cryptography offers a future-proof alternative.

24. Quantum Biology
For decades, biologists assumed quantum effects were irrelevant in the warm, wet, noisy environment of living cells. That assumption is now being challenged. Quantum biology is the emerging field studying whether quantum phenomena play functional roles in biological processes.

The evidence is growing. Photosynthesis in plants and bacteria may achieve its remarkable near-100% energy transfer efficiency through quantum coherence — a wavelike spread of excitation across multiple molecular pathways simultaneously. European robins appear to navigate using a quantum-mechanical radical pair mechanism in a protein called cryptochrome, effectively using entanglement to sense Earth’s magnetic field. Even enzyme action and DNA mutation may involve quantum tunneling. If confirmed, these findings would mean quantum mechanics is not just the physics of the very small — it’s woven into the fabric of life itself.

25. Quantum Gravity: The Final Frontier
Here is where physics confronts its greatest unsolved problem. General relativity — Einstein’s theory of gravity — describes spacetime as a smooth, continuous fabric curved by mass and energy. Quantum mechanics describes the universe as fundamentally discrete, probabilistic, and field-based. The two theories are spectacularly successful in their respective domains but are mathematically incompatible with each other.

Unifying them into a single theory of quantum gravity is the holy grail of modern theoretical physics. Two leading candidates are string theory (which proposes that all particles are vibrations of tiny one-dimensional strings, requiring extra spatial dimensions) and loop quantum gravity (which quantizes spacetime itself into discrete chunks called spin networks). Neither is experimentally confirmed. The difficulty is that quantum gravity effects are expected to manifest only at the Planck scale — energies roughly 10¹⁵ times higher than any particle accelerator we could build in the foreseeable future. Cracking this problem would not only unify physics but transform our understanding of the origin and ultimate fate of the universe.

—

Conclusion: Embracing the Weirdness
From the quantization of energy in 1900 to the quantum biology discoveries of today, the last century of physics has been a relentless assault on common sense. Particles are waves. Cats are alive and dead. Observation creates reality. Particles tunnel through walls, entangle across space, and may navigate birds across continents.

What unites all 25 of these mind-bending concepts is a single, radical lesson: the universe is not required to make sense to us. Our intuitions evolved to navigate a world of medium-sized objects moving at medium speeds — a narrow slice of physical reality. At the quantum level, those intuitions fail completely, and mathematics takes over as our most reliable guide.

Yet quantum physics works. It powers your electronics, enables modern medicine, and may soon transform computing and communication. The strangeness isn’t a bug in the theory — it’s a feature of the universe. And as physicists work toward quantum gravity, quantum biology, and practical quantum technologies, the strangest chapters of this story may still be ahead.

The quantum world doesn’t ask for your belief. It just asks you to keep looking.

—

Frequently Asked Questions
What is the most important concept in quantum physics?

Wave-particle duality and the uncertainty principle are arguably the most foundational, as they established that quantum objects fundamentally defy classical description. However, quantum entanglement is often considered the most remarkable for its implications about non-locality and the nature of reality.

Is quantum physics proven, or is it just a theory?

Quantum mechanics is among the most rigorously tested frameworks in all of science. Its predictions have been verified experimentally to extraordinary precision — the magnetic moment of the electron is predicted and measured to 12 decimal places. It underpins real technologies like lasers, MRI machines, transistors, and atomic clocks. In science, “theory” means a well-substantiated explanation, not a guess.

What does the observer effect actually mean — does a human have to observe something?

No. In quantum mechanics, “observation” means any physical interaction that leaves a record in the environment — a detector click, a change in another particle, anything that carries “which-path” information. Conscious human observation is not required. The measurement problem is partly about figuring out exactly what kind of interaction triggers wavefunction collapse.

What is the difference between the Copenhagen and Many-Worlds interpretations?

In the Copenhagen interpretation, the wavefunction collapses to a single definite outcome when a measurement is made. In the Many-Worlds interpretation, no collapse occurs — instead, the universe branches into multiple copies, each containing one of the possible outcomes. Both reproduce the same experimental predictions, so the disagreement is about what’s “really” happening, not about measurable consequences.

Could quantum tunneling ever be used for practical transportation?

No. Quantum tunneling scales inversely with mass — the probability of an object tunneling through a barrier drops exponentially as its mass increases. For subatomic particles it’s significant; for anything human-sized, the probability is so vanishingly small it’s effectively zero for all practical purposes. The technology that uses tunneling (transistors, scanning tunneling microscopes) works precisely because it operates at atomic scales.

What is quantum biology, and is it really proven?

Quantum biology is an emerging field, and the evidence varies by phenomenon. Quantum coherence in photosynthesis has strong experimental support (backed by research from groups at Berkeley, MIT, and elsewhere). The radical pair mechanism in bird navigation is well-supported theoretically with growing experimental backing. Quantum tunneling in enzyme reactions has solid evidence. The field is young, but it’s moved well beyond speculation into serious peer-reviewed science.

8 claims about hantavirus and 2026 cruise ship outbreak we've examined
Story by Rae Deng • 4mo
Reader interest in rumors about the family of viruses rose amid the 2026 Andes hantavirus cruise ship outbreak.
© Andres Gutierrez/Anadolu, accessed via Getty Images
Almost immediately after news broke of a hantavirus outbreak on the Dutch cruise ship MV Hondius in spring 2026, rumors about the family of viruses and the rare but dangerous diseases they can cause in humans began proliferating online. 

Health officials have assured the public that as of early May, the cruise ship's infectious cases — which were caused by the Andes strain of the virus — did not pose a high risk to the global population. Still, in the wake of the COVID-19 pandemic, the news of a deadly viral outbreak fueled speculation about past hantavirus cases and a supposed "prediction" that hantavirus would follow after "Corona." 

Here are eight rumors Snopes has looked into about hantavirus.

An extremely stable quantum gas offers a new lens on strongly interacting systems
Story by Science X staff • 1d • 2 min read


Interaction tuning between dipolar molecules via microwave dressing. Credit: arXiv (2025). DOI: 10.48550/arxiv.2505.08773
Interaction tuning between dipolar molecules via microwave dressing. Credit: arXiv (2025). DOI: 10.48550/arxiv.2505.08773
Molecular gases are a new form of artificial quantum matter. However, when these molecules collide, they are often lost extremely rapidly. Researchers from Radboud University and Columbia University have been able to suppress this collisional loss, paving the way for strongly interacting quantum matter. This new artificial quantum matter allows researchers to study quantum behavior relevant to electrons in real materials. Their results are published in Science.

Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Top Ghostwriting Services
Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Top Ghostwriting Services
nexusbookwriting.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Quantum gases of molecules are created by cooling to nanokelvin temperatures, only 0.000000001° above absolute zero. Under these conditions, quantum effects dominate, and the molecules behave collectively, forming a Bose-Einstein condensate in which many molecules share the same quantum state. This new artificial quantum matter acts as a controllable model material that allows researchers to study quantum behavior relevant to electrons in real materials.

Collisional loss
But unlike electrons in real materials, the molecules in these artificial quantum systems are easily lost when they collide. For ultracold molecules, collisional loss is extremely rapid and occurs in essentially every close encounter.

Researcher Tijn Karman said, "By controlling the molecular dipole moments with microwaves, researchers were able to suppress these losses by more than a factor of 10,000. In fact, the suppression was so great that only an upper bound, set by the single-molecule lifetime, could be measured in the lab of Sebastian Will at Columbia."

From quantum gas to quantum liquid
Reducing collisional loss allows researchers to create denser and more stable molecular gases. At the same time, microwave control induces strong, long-range interactions between molecules. Karman said, "This combination makes it possible to realize stable quantum liquids, where the interaction range is larger than the average distance between molecules. Such systems provide a new platform for exploring strongly interacting quantum matter that is difficult to achieve with atomic gases."

A week earlier, Karman published an article about how he was able to create highly ordered arrays of molecules, almost like molecules in an egg carton. Such ordered molecular arrays could provide a foundation for scaling up experiments with molecules as quantum bits.

More information: Weijun Yuan et al, Extreme loss suppression in an ultracold molecular gas with widely tunable dipolar interactions, Science (2026). DOI: 10.1126/science.adz0521. On arXiv: DOI: 10.48550/arxiv.2505.08773

Did Pope Leo XIV say people should be 'less fearful of Islam'? Here's the full story
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 5mo • 4 min read


Pope Leo XIV speaks to journalists aboard a plane. Baris Seckin, accessed via Getty Images.
Pope Leo XIV speaks to journalists aboard a plane. Baris Seckin, accessed via Getty Images.

Claim:

Pope Leo XIV said “We should perhaps be a little less fearful of Islam.”

Rating:

Mostly True (About this rating?)

What's True:

Using Lebanon as an example of Muslims and Christians coexisting, Pope Leo XIV said in December 2025, “I think those are lessons that would be important also to be heard in Europe or North America, that we perhaps should be a little less fearful and look for ways of promoting authentic dialogue and respect.”

 
What's False:

Pope Leo did not use the exact words that circulated in social media posts. He was talking broadly about building bridges between Christians and Muslims as he responded to a question about Catholic people who perceive Islam as a threat.

 
In April 2026, after U.S. President Donald Trump made a Truth Social post criticizing Pope Leo XIV over comments condemning the U.S.-Israeli war with Iran, many online shared a purported quote by the pope. Per numerous posts on Facebook and X, the Pope allegedly said, "We should perhaps be a little less fearful of Islam."

Many of the internet users who shared the quote appeared to do so in support of Trump, sometimes alongside alongside images purportedly showing Islamic State militants beheading Christians. 


Did Pope Leo XIV say people should be 'less fearful of Islam'? Here's the full story
Did Pope Leo XIV say people should be 'less fearful of Islam'? Here's the full story
(Facebook user "David J Harris Jr.﻿")

Searches of the quote on Google (archived), Bing (archived) and DuckDuckGo (archived) found no evidence that the pope said the exact words above, but did show that in December 2025 he made a similar statement about how people should be "less fearful" while responding to a question about Catholics who believe Islam is a threat. Earlier in his statement, he said such "fears" are "generated by people who are against immigration and trying to keep out people who may be from another country, another religion, another race." As such, we rate this claim mostly true.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

The pope made the statement on Dec. 2, 2025, during a press conference on a flight. His comments, along with the question that prompted them, can be heard starting at the 17:02 mark of the following video published to YouTube by Vatican News, the official news portal for the Holy See.

Scientists discovered a nuclear island that flies in the face of traditional chemistry
Story by Darren Orf • 8mo • 3 min read

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this study:

While scientists have a pretty good handle on how protons and neutrons form stable nuclei, there are exceptions to those well-established rules.
Known as “Islands of Inversion,” these areas are regions where spherical shapes collapse and deformed objects reign, and typically, they’re found in neutron-rich isotopes.
A new study found what it calls an “Isospin-Symmetric Island of Inversion” in a stable region on the proton-rich side of the atomic stability line, offering new understanding regarding how atoms form.
In 1911, New Zealand physicist Ernest Rutherford theorized that atoms contained a nucleus, and in the ensuing century, scientists have learned a lot about the ins and outs of that nucleus while filling out the periodic table with 118 atomic elements.

Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematics Theory&Application - Mathematical Theory &Analysis
Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematics Theory&Application - Mathematical Theory &Analysis
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
It turns out atomic nuclei follow some simple rules. For example, when it comes to light nuclei (a.k.a. elements with smaller numbers on the periodic table), having the same number of neutrons and protons increases stability and forms what is known as the N=Z line (“N” being neutrons and “Z” being protons). However, as those numbers grow, the repulsive electromagnetic forces in protons require the presence of an increasing number of neutrons for stability (if there aren’t enough neutrons, the protons will repel each other and push the nucleus apart), resulting in a deviation from this line.

That said, over the years, scientists have also noticed a few behavioral quirks known as “islands of inversion,” where the usual rules of nuclear structure stop working. In other words, “magic numbers”—the counts of protons and neutrons that form stable nuclei—disappear, giving way to unusually strong deformations (how much the shape of the nucleus varies from spherical).

Related video: Nuclear fission sounds simple: Split an atom – "The physics behind it is absolutely wild" (Today I Found Out)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Today I Found Out
Nuclear fission sounds simple: Split an atom – "The physics behind it is absolutely wild"
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
0:00
100
Most of these “islands” occur in exotic isotopes like beryllium-12, magnesium-32, and chromium-64, which are all pretty distant from ‘normal’ nuclei found in nature and are on the neutron-rich side of the stability line. For instance, the most common isotope of chromium is chromium-52, which contains 24 protons and 28 neutrons. Chromium-64, on the other hand, contains 24 protons and a whopping 40 neutrons.

But in a new international study, scientists discovered a remarkably proton-rich island of inversion (with symmetrical proton and neutron excitations) by examining two isotopes of molybdenum: Mo-84 (Z=N=42) and Mo-86 (Z=42, Z=44). They found that the difference of just two neutrons caused Mo-84 to experience what’s called “particle-hole excitation,” where nucleons jump to higher-energy orbitals and leave vacancies in their wake.

Because this behavior occurs in a stable region, where the number of protons approximately equals the number of neutrons, this discovery challenges where certain “islands of inversion” may appear while providing a new look into how nuclei bind together. The results of the study were published in the journal Nature Communications.

Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
“While this phenomenon has been so far studied in very neutron-rich nuclei, its manifestation in nuclei residing on the proton-rich side remains to be explored in detail due to the experimental difficulty of producing medium-heavy-mass N ~ Z nuclei,” the authors wrote. “The two isotopes [reveal] a profound change in their structure and affords deeper insight into the evolution of the nuclear structure at the proton-rich side of the stability line.”


This graph shows the N=Z line as well as the stability link below. The green areas represent other islands on inversion on the neutron-rich side of the stability line where as the purple ellipsoid represents the isospin-symetric island of inversion featured in this study.
This graph shows the N=Z line as well as the stability link below. The green areas represent other islands on inversion on the neutron-rich side of the stability line where as the purple ellipsoid represents the isospin-symetric island of inversion featured in this study.
© Nature Communications (2025)
Almost as complicated as the discovery itself are the methods that the scientists used to make it. First, the team bombarded a beryllium target with accelerated Mo-92 ions, and separated the desired fragments out after the collisions. When resulting Mo-86 atoms struck a second target, some were excited into Mo-84, emitting gamma rays in the process. Those gamma rays were then measured by the GRETINA gamma-ray spectrometer and the TRIPLEX (Triple Plunger for Exotic beams) instrument, both of which can record extremely short atomic lifetimes. The resulting data allowed scientists to discern nuclear deformation.

This new “Isospin-Symmetric Island of Inversion” shows that while we’ve made a lot of progress since Rutherford’s experiments in the early 20th century, there are still many nuclear mysteries out there waiting to be solved.

Did Pope Leo XIV say he'd probably cheer for Peru over US in FIFA World Cup?
Story by Jordan Liles • 4mo • 5 min read


A photo shows Pope Leo XIV wearing white, including a white head covering, and giving a thumbs up to a crowd. Alberto Pizzoli, accessed via Getty Images
A photo shows Pope Leo XIV wearing white, including a white head covering, and giving a thumbs up to a crowd. Alberto Pizzoli, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

Pope Leo XIV said he would "probably" cheer for Peru over the U.S. should the two teams compete in the FIFA World Cup.

Rating:

Correct Attribution (About this rating?)

Context:

Leo is a dual citizen of the U.S. and Peru, and served in Peru for much of his life as a missionary and then a bishop. Social media users only shared the very beginning of his longer answer from a July 2025 interview, in which Leo added of all sports "as pope I'm a fan of all the teams."

 
In late April and early May 2026, social media users alleged Pope Leo XIV said in an interview he would "probably" cheer for Peru over the U.S. should the two teams compete in the FIFA World Cup.

In short, Leo truly said this. In the same answer, he also said of all sports that "as pope I'm a fan of all the teams."

Leo said "probably Peru" during a July 2025 video interview with Elise Ann Allen, a Leo biographer and senior correspondent for Crux, a website providing independent coverage of the Vatican and the Catholic Church.

Users shared very brief clips — or partial transcripts — featuring only the "probably Peru" part of Leo's answer, omitting his complete thoughts on the matter.


Leo is a dual citizen of the U.S. and Peru, and served in Peru for much of his life as a missionary and later a bishop.

Peru failed to qualify for the 2026 FIFA World Cup, as did Italy — another country Leo mentioned in his full answer, which appears below.

Leo's complete World Cup answer
In September 2025, Crux published a YouTube video containing Allen's World Cup question and Leo's complete answer, as well as a partial transcript of the multi-part interview.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

At the beginning of Allen's first interview video, she asks if Leo feels more like he's the first pope from the U.S. or the second representing Latin America, including, "Do you feel more American? Do you feel more Latin American?" Leo answers, splitting his admiration of both the U.S. and Peru much in the same way he split up his life living in the two countries:

LEO: I think the answer is both and, one of the things that has come out in a number of articles that have been published as journalists and others try to decipher, interpret or make up what they think I am, but there's an element of truth in statements that are being made about the world experience that I've had. And even the studies that have done and the family tree genealogy and finding. I know some of what's been published but a lot has been discovered that I wasn't aware of, and there seems to be something someone said to me recently between the family background of all the different nationalities including my brother, who just got a book that was published in France talking about the African roots, which there apparently are, and French and Italian and Spanish and Cuban and Haitian, Peruvian, because of the Spanish connection.

All of that together has, along with my experience especially as prior generally in getting to know the church around the world, has given me, I think, a real appreciation for the wonderful wealth of traditions and cultures and languages that make up the world and, of course, the church.

So, I'm obviously an American and I very much feel that I'm an American, but I also love very much Peru, the Peruvian people, and so that is a part of who I am. Half of my ministerial life was spent in Peru, so the Latin American perspective is very valuable to me and I think comes out also in an appreciation that I have for the life of the church from Latin America which I believe was significant in both my connection with Pope Francis, my understanding of some of the vision that Pope Francis had for the church and how in some ways, at least, we can continue to carry that on from what Francis was, promote. That word's not promote. Francis was offering to the church in terms of a vision, a true prophetic vision for the church today and tomorrow.

Some objects we thought were planets may actually be tiny black holes from the dawn of time
Story by Paul Sutter • 8mo • 3 min read


A telescope image of 32 planet-forming discs around young stars. New research hints that some exoplanet discoveries may actually be primordial black holes in disguise.
A telescope image of 32 planet-forming discs around young stars. New research hints that some exoplanet discoveries may actually be primordial black holes in disguise.
© ESO, ESA/Gaia/DPAC, M. Vioque et al.
What if some of the alien worlds we've discovered are not actually planets at all?

Astronomers have spent years cataloging thousands of worlds orbiting distant stars, assuming that if something has the mass of a planet and exerts a gravitational pull on its parent star, it must be a planet.

zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
But there may be a ghostly alternative lurking in the early universe. In a recent paper that was uploaded to the arXiv preprint server but has not been peer-reviewed, researchers suggest that some "exoplanets" we've detected might actually be something far more exotic — primordial black holes.

These are not your garden-variety black holes, born from dying stars. Instead, they are hypothetical leftovers from the Big Bang itself, formed when the newborn universe was a chaotic, high-pressure soup of energy. These "mini" black holes could have the mass of Earth or Jupiter but be the size of a grapefruit.

Our current methods for finding planets are exceptionally good at measuring mass but less so at determining the physical size of a planet. For example, we often use the radial velocity method — a technique that involves watching a star "wobble" because the gravity of an orbiting object is yanking on it. If the wobble is big, the object is heavy. If the wobble is small, the object is light.

Related video: Watch what scientists found beyond our solar system - it wasn't supposed to exist (The Space Race)
View on Watch
View on Watch

The Space Race
Watch what scientists found beyond our solar system - it wasn't supposed to exist
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
14:16
0
But here's the catch: A planet with the mass of Neptune and a black hole with the mass of Neptune produce the exact same wobble.

In an attempt to separate the two, the authors of the new study looked at exoplanets that have been detected via these wobbles but have never been seen crossing the face of their star — a process called a transit. When a planet transits, it blocks some light, telling us its physical size. If an object pulls on a star but never blocks any light, it might be because it is too small to see, or it might be because it is a black hole.

The researchers identified several intriguing suspects, including Kepler-21 Ac, HD 219134 f and Wolf 1061 d. These objects are heavy enough to make their stars wobble, yet they remain invisible to our telescopes. The team pointed to microlensing events — brief flashes of light caused when a massive object passes in front of a distant star and acts like a magnifying glass — as potential hiding spots for these ancient nomads.

Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The authors admitted that these candidates are merely representative possibilities, rather than a definitive gallery of tiny black holes. Most will likely turn out to be ordinary planets that just happen to have tilted orbits that prevent them from transiting.

The next decade of data from missions like the Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope — a NASA telescope that will take a broad survey of exoplanets, due to launch as soon as this fall — will be crucial for learning more about these objects. We might catch one evaporating via Hawking radiation, a theoretical process whereby black holes slowly leak energy until they vanish. If so, we might discover that the universe is a lot more crowded with ancient black holes than we ever imagined.

Has the Treasury declared the US 'insolvent'? We set the record straight
Story by Jack Izzo & Anna Rascouët-Paz • 5mo • 10 min read


One-dollar, 25-cent, 10-cent and one-cent coins pile up on a U.S. flag. Image courtesy of Jira Hera, accessed via Canva
One-dollar, 25-cent, 10-cent and one-cent coins pile up on a U.S. flag. Image courtesy of Jira Hera, accessed via Canva

Claim:

In March 2026, the U.S. Department of the Treasury declared the U.S. government insolvent.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

Following the U.S. Department of the Treasury's publication of financial documents for the previous fiscal year on March 16, 2026, rumors abounded that it had declared the U.S. government insolvent — meaning the Treasury purportedly announced the government could no longer repay its debts. 

An X user shared the rumor in a March 28, 2026, post (archived), adding that the government had allegedly spent $1.8 trillion more than it had collected in the year ending on Sept. 30, 2025.



Other users shared the claim in posts on Facebook, X and Threads. One post (archived) called the news "major" and lamented that no media outlet was reporting on the alleged development. Several Snopes readers also searched the website and emailed us, seeking to confirm whether the rumor was true. 

The claim stemmed from an opinion piece in Fortune magazine on March 23, 2026. While the original Fortune article was tagged as "commentary" on the publication's website, the piece also appeared on Yahoo Finance. There, the only indication the piece was opinion, not fact, was at the very bottom of the article.

Its author, Steve Hanke, an economist at Johns Hopkins University, asserted that the Treasury had "declared the U.S. insolvent," citing figures from the Treasury's financial report and statements:

That's not hyperbole — it's the conclusion drawn directly from the Treasury Department's own consolidated financial statements for fiscal year 2025, released last week to near-total media silence. The numbers: $6.06 trillion in total assets against $47.78 trillion in total liabilities as of September 30, 2025.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Hanke then asked readers to imagine what the federal budget would look like at the scale of one household:

So consider this: divide every number by 100 million — drop eight zeros — and federal finances look like a household budget in freefall.

That household earns $52,446 and spends $73,378 — running a $20,932 annual deficit. Its total liabilities and unfunded promises amount to $1,361,788 against just $60,554 in assets, leaving it $1.3 million in the hole. Uncle Sam, by any accounting standard, is insolvent.

Why Quantum Mechanics Means Reality Is Far Stranger Than Thought
©Photo by Brecht Corbeel on Unsplash
Most people view the universe as a solid, predictable place governed by strict physical rules. We believe objects exist in one specific place whether someone looks or not.

Space-time doesn't exist, but it's a useful framework for understanding our reality
Story by Daryl Janzen • 11mo • 5 min read


Credit: CC0 Public Domain
Credit: CC0 Public Domain
Whether space-time exists should be neither controversial nor even conceptually challenging, given the definitions of "space-time," "events" and "instants." The idea that space-time exists is no more viable than the outdated belief that the celestial sphere exists: both are observer-centered models that are powerful and convenient for describing the world, but neither represents reality itself.

Introduction To Topology
Introduction To Topology
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Yet from the standpoints of modern physics, philosophy, popular science communication and familiar themes in science fiction, stating that space-time does not exist is contentious.

But what would it mean for a world where everything that has ever happened or will happen somehow "exists" now as part of an interwoven fabric?

Events are not locations
It's easy to imagine past events—like losing a tooth or receiving good news—as existing somewhere. Fictional representations of time travel underscore this: time travelers alter events and disrupt the timeline, as if past and future events were locations one could visit with the right technology.

Related video: Why time doesn't really exist, according to physics (Bright Side)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Bright Side
Why time doesn't really exist, according to physics
Time runs like a river.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
4:28
0
Philosophers often talk this way too. Eternalism says all events across all time exist. The growing block view suggests the past and present exist while the future will come to be. Presentism says only the present exists, while the past used to exist and the future will when it happens. And general relativity presents a four-dimensional continuum that bends and curves—we tend to imagine that continuum of events as really existing.

The confusion emerges out of the definition of the word "exist." With space-time, it's applied uncritically to a mathematical description of happenings—turning a model into an ontological theory on the nature of being.

A totality
In physics, space-time is the continuous set of events that happen throughout space and time—from here to the furthest galaxy, from the Big Bang to the far future. It is a four-dimensional map that records and measures where and when everything happens. In physics, an event is an instantaneous occurrence at a specific place and time.

zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
An instant is the three-dimensional collection of spatially separated events that happen "at the same time" (with relativity's usual caveat that simultaneity depends on one's relative state of rest).

Space-time is the totality of all events that ever happen.

It's also our most powerful way of cataloging the world's happenings. That cataloging is indispensable, but the words and concepts we use for it matter.

There are infinitely many points in the three dimensions of space, and at every instant as time passes a unique event occurs at each location.

Positionings throughout time
Physicists describe a car traveling straight at constant speed with a simple space-time diagram: position on one axis, time on the other. Instants stack together to form a two-dimensional space-time. The car's position is a point within each instant, and those points join to form a worldline—the full record of the car's position throughout the time interval, whose slope is the car's speed.

AI Ethics: A Textbook (Artificial Intelligence: Foundations, Theory, And Algorithms)
AI Ethics: A Textbook (Artificial Intelligence: Foundations, Theory, And Algorithms)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Real motion is far more complex. The car rides along on a rotating Earth orbiting the sun, which orbits the Milky Way as it drifts through the local universe. Plotting the car's position at every instant ultimately requires four-dimensional space-time.

Space-time is the map of where and when events happen. A worldline is the record of every event that occurs throughout one's life. The key question is whether the map—or all the events it draws together at once—should be said to exist in the same way that cars, people and the places they go exist.

Objects exist
Consider what "exist" means. Objects, buildings, people, cities, planets, galaxies exist—they are either places or occupy places, enduring there over intervals of time. They persist through changes and can be encountered repeatedly.

Treating occurrences as things that exist smuggles confusion into our language and concepts. When analyzing space-time, do events, instants, worldlines or even space-time as a whole exist in the same sense as places and people? Or is it more accurate to say that events happen in an existing world?

In that view, space-time is the map that records those happenings, allowing us to describe the spatial and temporal relationships between them.

Space-time does not exist
Events do not exist, they happen. Consequently, space-time does not exist. Events happen everywhere throughout the course of existence, and the occurrence of an event is categorically different from the existence of anything—whether object, place or concept.

First, there is no empirical evidence that any past, present or future event "exists" in the way that things in the world around us exist. Verifying the existence of an event as an ongoing object would require something like a time machine to go and observe it now. Even present events cannot be verified as ongoing things that exist.

In contrast, material objects exist. Time-travel paradoxes rest on the false premise that events exist as revisitable locations. Recognizing the categorical difference between occurrence and existence resolves these paradoxes.

Second, this recognition reframes the philosophy of time. Much debate over the past century has treated events as things that exist. Philosophers then focus on their tense properties: is an event past, present or future? Did this one occur earlier or later than that one?

These discussions rely on an assumption that events are existent things that bear these properties. From there, it's a short step to the conclusion that time is unreal or that the passage of time is an illusion, on the identification that the same event can be labeled differently from different standpoints. But the ontological distinction was lost at the start: events don't exist, they happen. Tense and order are features of how happenings relate within an existing world, not properties of existent objects.

Finally, consider relativity. It is a mathematical theory that describes a four-dimensional space-time continuum, and not a theory about a four-dimensional thing that exists—that, in the course of its own existence, bends and warps due to gravity.

Conceptual clarity
Physics can't actually describe space-time itself as something that actually exists, nor can it account for any change it might experience as an existing thing.

Space-time provides a powerful description of how events happen: how they are ordered relative to one another, how sequences of events are measured to unfold and how lengths are measured in different reference frames. If we stop saying that events—and space-time—exist, we recover conceptual clarity without sacrificing a single prediction.

An AI 'kill switch' could go as far as shutting down the internet
Story by Greta Reich, USA TODAY • 4h • 6 min read


Detroit Free Press
OpenAI CEO Sam Altman warns of AI risks, urges global caution at tech conference
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
1:29
0
Amid a wave of industry leaders and policymakers urging one another to regulate and decelerate the rapid progress of artificial intelligence, the idea of an AI “kill switch” is growing more prominent.  

A “kill switch” for AI is a catch-all phrase for any type of guardrail that could prevent or disrupt AI agents from taking unwanted action. It includes software and hardware interference.  

Members of Congress from both parties have introduced “kill switch” bills that would require AI developers to maintain the ability to suspend or shut down their products, though some Senate Republicans remain opposed. 

Start the day smarter. Get all the news you need in your inbox each morning.

Even Anthropic co-founder Jack Clark floated the idea of mandatory "kill switches" for AI systems, telling BBC on Sept. 14, “I think that’s the kind of thing society is going to want to know and might want to eventually pass rules around.”  


A flag reading " STOP THE AI RACE" flutters during a rally outside of the OpenAI headquarters, as protesters call for a pause in AI development, in San Francisco, California, U.S., March 21, 2026. REUTERS/Manuel Orbegozo
A flag reading " STOP THE AI RACE" flutters during a rally outside of the OpenAI headquarters, as protesters call for a pause in AI development, in San Francisco, California, U.S., March 21, 2026. REUTERS/Manuel Orbegozo
© Manuel Orbegozo, REUTERS
But what exactly a “kill switch” would look like – and the likelihood of it being effectively implemented – is still widely debated. 

More: Many in Congress want to rein in AI. They're split on whether to hurry.

What is a ‘kill switch’? 
The definition of a “kill switch” depends on the scale at which one plans to use it.  

“At a very local level, like a building or data center or something like that, it could be trivially easy,” said Michael Vermeer, a physical scientist at nonpartisan research organization RAND. “It could be literally pulling a plug, cutting the power, blocking internet access, something like that.” 

On a global level, it might go as far as shutting down the internet.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

In either scenario, the goal is to revoke an AI agent’s access to some system it shouldn’t have access to, or inversely, have a human manually approve access.  

This could include hardware solutions like turning off the power to a model’s servers or building restraints into a chip – the small electronic circuits that AI models run on – or software solutions like isolating an agent so it can’t access certain systems. It could also mean learning to interpret a model to prevent bad action before it occurs.

Physicists build a 'mad-scientist' antimatter beam to test Einstein's theory of general relativity
Story by Michelle Starr • 1d • 5 min read


The particle accelerator at the Paul Scherrer Institute in Villigen, Switzerland, used to carry out the experiments. (Markus Fischer/Paul Scherrer Institute)
The particle accelerator at the Paul Scherrer Institute in Villigen, Switzerland, used to carry out the experiments. (Markus Fischer/Paul Scherrer Institute)
According to Einstein's theory of general relativity, gravity does not discriminate.

Matter, antimatter – it's all the same as far as gravity is concerned, and it all should fall in exactly the same way.

So far, scientific investigations into this phenomenon broadly agree. Gravity doesn't care what you're made of – it finds you attractive regardless.

Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Team of Bestsellers
Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Team of Bestsellers
nexusbookwriting.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Well, sorry friends, but physics doesn't make the rules; it just describes them. In this case, gravity's universal approach to attraction is known as the weak equivalence principle.

There are some regimes that have been a bit too wacky to test. But now physicists have opened the door to a new probe in the antimatter regime.

They've built and tested a muonium beam – a stream of exotic, antimatter-containing 'atoms' that could finally let physicists watch how gravity acts on them before they disappear.

"We have taken an important step towards carrying out an exciting experiment on this topic," says physicist Anna Soter of ETH Zürich, corresponding author of a paper published in Nature Physics.

"We want to measure the gravitational interaction of the muon."

To do that, however, the researchers need muonium – a bizarre, short-lived atom-like particle consisting of a positively charged antimuon paired with a negatively charged electron, making the whole thing electrically neutral.


YouTube Thumbnail
YouTube Thumbnail
The antimuon is the antimatter counterpart of the muon, a fundamental particle similar to an electron but around 200 times more massive. That means almost all of muonium's mass comes from its antimatter component.

This makes muonium very different from the antimatter physicists have already subjected to gravity.

Related video: Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed
Hello mortals.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
21:10
0
In 2023, CERN's ALPHA experiment showed that antihydrogen – an antiproton paired with a positron – falls towards Earth in a way consistent with ordinary matter.

That was pretty groundbreaking, but protons and antiprotons aren't fundamental particles. They're composite objects made of quarks. The antimuon, on the other hand, is an elementary particle belonging to the second generation of fundamental particles.

So a gravity experiment involving muonium would enter free-fall territory that no other experiment has been able to probe.

"The exotic muonium is very well suited to this because it is a neutral atom," Soter explains. "After all, to make something fall, you need something neutral."

There's just one tiny little kink in the hose.

Muonium only lasts for about 2.2 microseconds before it decays. That's not a lot of time in which to take a measurement.

Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
But what muonium lacks in longevity, the researchers figured they could compensate for with volume – a veritable firehose of muonium particles, traveling in a tightly controlled beam, mostly in the same direction and at roughly the same speed.

"As muonium atoms have such a short lifetime, we need to start with a large number so that enough survive long enough to be measured after falling for a few microseconds," Soter says.

Existing muonium sources aren't very good at that. They produce diffuse "thermal" beams, spraying atoms over a wide range of angles and velocities. So the researchers came up with another plan – firing antimuons into a layer of superfluid helium cooled to about 0.2 Kelvin.

When the antimuons enter the helium, they rapidly lose energy, coming to a stop just beneath its surface. There, an antimuon can pick up an electron that was left in its wake as it blasted through the helium, forming muonium.


Positive muons enter superfluid helium, where they form muonium that emerges into vacuum as a narrow, superthermal beam. (Tang & Zhao, Nat. Phys. , 2026)
Positive muons enter superfluid helium, where they form muonium that emerges into vacuum as a narrow, superthermal beam. (Tang & Zhao, Nat. Phys. , 2026)
Then the superfluid does something extremely useful.

Muonium doesn't really like being inside superfluid helium. The interaction between the two gives the muonium extra energy, effectively driving the newly formed 'atoms' towards the surface.

When they reach it, they burst out into the vacuum above at a speed determined largely by that energy boost – in contrast to the random thermal motion that makes conventional muonium sources such a hot mess.

The result is a remarkably orderly stream of muonium, shooting out at around 2.2 kilometers per second with a much narrower velocity spread. Around 8 percent of the antimuons sent into the helium ultimately emerged into the vacuum as muonium.


The experimental setup used to generate and detect the new superthermal muonium beam. (Zhang et al., Nat. Phys. , 2026)
The experimental setup used to generate and detect the new superthermal muonium beam. (Zhang et al., Nat. Phys. , 2026)
Detectors positioned above the helium allowed the researchers to reconstruct the paths of the muonium atoms from the particles produced as they decayed. Those measurements revealed the narrow, fast-moving beam they had hoped to produce.

But the experiment isn't quite ready to measure what gravity does to the particles.

zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
zee quantum field theory - Amazon Official Site
amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
For one thing, the beam is currently pointing in the wrong direction.

The superfluid helium spits the muonium vertically upwards. To measure its acceleration under gravity, the researchers need the particles to travel horizontally, giving gravity a chance to tug them ever so slightly downward during their fleeting journey.

The team is already working on a way to turn the beam sideways. The next piece of the puzzle is an interferometer that can measure that incredibly tiny gravitational displacement.

The proposed device would send the muonium through three extremely fine gratings spaced just millimeters apart. Thanks to the weirdness of quantum mechanics, the particles also behave like waves, producing an interference pattern as they pass through.


Subscribe to ScienceAlert's free fact-checked newsletter
Subscribe to ScienceAlert's free fact-checked newsletter
If gravity acts on muonium as expected, its downward pull should shift that pattern by a minuscule amount. By measuring the shift, the researchers can determine muonium's gravitational acceleration.

With their new beam, the researchers estimate they could eventually measure that acceleration to about 1 percent.

Related: Physicists Just Found a Quirk in Einstein's Predictions of Spacetime

They hope to test the method with the atomic beam by the end of this year, with the actual gravity experiment potentially following within two or three years.

Then, we'll see whether muonium misbehaves under gravity. If so, it could point to the existence of a fifth physical force in the Universe. But even if not, Soter says, her team will have done something magnificent.

"I simply want to measure, for the first time, whether the equivalence between gravitational and inertial mass also applies to the second generation of particles – this alone is quite an inspiring piece of work," she says.

Poor old Einstein – dead for more than 70 years, and he still has to take exams.

The research has been published in Nature Physics.

The new electric BMW M3 is already in for the fight of its life
Story by Joel Stocksdale • 23h • 3 min read

BMW's electric M3, likely called the iM3, is coming quite soon. The brand showed off a near-production example a couple of months ago, and testing is continuing at the Nürburgring, as shown by these spy photos. While it would be easy to assume that the iM3 will be on par with the eventual gas-powered M3 in performance, it's entirely possible this electric BMW will far exceed it, as the fastest EVs at the 'Ring are sedans, too.

In fact, the driver of the iM3 in these photos was pushing it hard to stay in front of a recent record-holder.


CarBuzz / Valnet
CarBuzz / Valnet
These photos show the iM3 at a corner of the 'Ring that you may recognize from highlight reels on YouTube by channels such as AutoAddiction. It's not uncommon for drivers to get a little sideways and sometimes end up in the gravel. On entry, the iM3 looked pretty stable and planted, as you can see in the first few photos from our spy photographer.

We also get a solid look at some of the iM3's defining details thanks to the nice lighting. The pointy hood and big heat-extracting vent and punched-up fenders really stand out.


CarBuzz / Valnet
CarBuzz / Valnet
About halfway through the corner, though, it's clear that the driver was carrying a little extra speed as the weight shifts and some countersteer starts coming in. We also get a better look at the back, which shows the tail looking a little more restrained than on the concept with a more subtle rear spoiler and diffuser.


CarBuzz / Valnet
CarBuzz / Valnet
A hint of tire smoke haze and some fresh tire tracks show that the driver was getting the wheels spun up. So even though the driver wasn't using a huge amount of opposite lock, they were working the car pretty hard.

Daily Galaxy
84.4K Followers
New theory of gravity could change everything we know about the cosmos
Story by Lydia Amazouz • 8mo • 4 min read


Credit: Shutterstock | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
Credit: Shutterstock | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
The universe’s accelerating expansion has long puzzled physicists. For decades, dark energy, an unseen force thought to make up nearly 70% of the cosmos, has been the leading explanation. But a new study published in the Journal of Cosmology and Astroparticle Physics suggests that this mysterious ingredient might not be necessary at all. Scientists from the Center of Applied Space Technology and Microgravity (ZARM) at the University of Bremen, working with colleagues from the Transylvanian University of Brașov, propose that the acceleration of the universe could naturally arise from an extended version of Einstein’s general theory of relativity, offering a new geometric perspective on the cosmos.

Mathematics Theory&Application - Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematical Theory &Analysis
Mathematics Theory&Application - Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematical Theory &Analysis
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The Cosmic Puzzle Of Accelerated Expansion
Since the 1990s, astronomical observations, such as those from supernovae and the cosmic microwave background, have shown that the universe is not only expanding but doing so at an accelerating rate. Within the standard model of cosmology, this phenomenon is explained by introducing dark energy into Einstein’s Friedmann equations, which describe how the universe evolves over time. Yet, dark energy remains an unobservable construct, a mathematical patch added to fit the data rather than a substance derived from first principles.


Ai Generated Picture Of The Expansion Of The Universe 777x777
Ai Generated Picture Of The Expansion Of The Universe 777x777
© Daily Galaxy US
The ZARM research team sought to challenge this conceptual shortcut. They questioned whether the apparent acceleration might emerge from geometry itself, rather than from an external force. Their investigation centered on Finsler gravity, an extension of Einstein’s general relativity that modifies the very structure of spacetime. This theoretical framework allows for a broader description of how space and time interact with matter and energy, potentially eliminating the need to invoke dark energy as a separate entity.

Related video: The universe has been dying since the beginning - scientists finally know why (Naked Science)

View on Watch

Naked Science
The universe has been dying since the beginning - scientists finally know why
Current Time

A New Framework: Finsler Gravity And The Friedmann Equations
The researchers’ approach modifies the mathematical foundation of cosmology. In the traditional Einstein-Friedmann model, cosmic acceleration cannot occur without inserting an additional term representing dark energy. But when the team replaced general relativity with Finsler gravity, the modified Finsler-Friedmann equations naturally predicted an accelerating universe, even in the absence of dark energy.

“This is an exciting indication that we may be able to explain the accelerated expansion of the universe, at least in parts, without dark energy, on the basis of a generalized spacetime geometry,” says Christian Pfeifer, ZARM physicist and member of the research team. “This new geometric point of view on the dark energy problem opens up new possibilities for better understanding the laws of nature in the cosmos.”

The study, appearing in the Journal of Cosmology and Astroparticle Physics, provides a theoretical alternative that could reconcile cosmic acceleration with Einstein’s equations, but under a more general and flexible geometry. The findings imply that what we perceive as the influence of dark energy might instead be a manifestation of how spacetime itself behaves at large scales.

Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Implications For Modern Cosmology
If validated, this new interpretation could reshape one of the most fundamental aspects of cosmology. Removing the need for dark energy would mean revising the ΛCDM model, which currently serves as the standard description of the universe. Such a paradigm shift would have profound implications for cosmic evolution, the fate of the universe, and our understanding of gravity itself.

The researchers emphasize that this work does not completely eliminate the possibility of dark energy but suggests that it might not be the sole driver of cosmic acceleration. By grounding the acceleration in the geometry of spacetime, Finsler gravity challenges the necessity of an unknown “energy field” and instead attributes the universe’s expansion to the inherent properties of the cosmos.

Future observational tests, through precision cosmology, gravitational lensing, or cosmic background radiation mapping, could determine whether this geometric model accurately describes reality. If confirmed, it could mark a turning point in theoretical physics, bringing us one step closer to unifying general relativity and quantum mechanics under a single coherent framework.

Introduction To Topology
Introduction To Topology
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
A New Geometric Vision Of The Universe
The work of Pfeifer and his colleagues highlights a profound shift in how scientists might conceptualize the cosmos. It invites a return to geometry, not as a passive backdrop to physical events, but as a dynamic entity capable of shaping the evolution of the universe itself. Such an approach echoes Einstein’s original intuition that gravity is not a force in the conventional sense but a manifestation of spacetime curvature.

By expanding this curvature into the more flexible framework of Finsler geometry, researchers open the door to a universe where acceleration is a built-in feature of cosmic structure, not a symptom of an invisible substance. This theoretical leap could eventually lead to a deeper understanding of why the universe behaves as it does, and perhaps even reveal new connections between gravity, matter, and the fabric of reality.
