Did Kurt Russell say, 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents'?
Story by Jordan Liles • 1mo • 2 min read


An image shows a close-up of actor Kurt Russell wearing a black bow tie, black suit, white dress shirt and tinted sunglasses at a red carpet-style event. Valery Hache accessed via Getty Images
An image shows a close-up of actor Kurt Russell wearing a black bow tie, black suit, white dress shirt and tinted sunglasses at a red carpet-style event. Valery Hache accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

Actor Kurt Russell once said, "Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents."

Rating:

Incorrect Attribution (About this rating?)

In July 2026, people claimed film and TV actor Kurt Russell once said, "Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents."

For example, on July 24, David J. Harris Jr., who hosts the Newsmax TV show "The Pulse," posted on Facebook (archived) a photo of Russell that displayed the word "BREAKING" with two police car light emojis. The post's text read, "BREAKING: Famous actor, Kurt Russell, came out STRONG against woke parents. He says: 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents.'" Harris made the same exact post (archived) on the previous day.


Did Kurt Russell say, 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents'?
Did Kurt Russell say, 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents'?
(David J. Harris Jr. accessed via Facebook)

Other users shared the same alleged quote on Facebook (archived), Instagram (archived) and X (archived).

In short, Russell did not say this. Users shared a fabricated quote without presenting evidence of Russell ever making the remark.

Snopes reached out by email to a Russell representatives with United Talent. We asked for official confirmation that Russell never said the alleged quote and will update this article if we receive that confirmation.

Researching the quote
Searches of DuckDuckGo and Google failed to locate any news media outlets reporting Russell made the remark, "Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents." Entertainment and political outlets would have widely reported on the politically charged matter had he truly made the comment.

Additional searches for the quote on the online book archive Google Books, newspaper archive Newspapers.com and YouTube found no further details, either.

Those searches, as well as reverse image searches, located several memes containing the quote on Facebook (archived) Instagram (archived), Threads (archived) and X (archived). According to the searches, the earliest posts attributing the quote to Russell dated back to at least May 2026. We have not yet confirmed the original source of the quote.

Months earlier in March, users shared a similar rumor falsely alleging people boycotted Russell after he said children should not be allowed to watch cartoons with LGBTQ+ themes. No evidence supported the claim Russell made that remark, either.

For further reading, we previously reported the truth of whether U.S. President Donald Trump celebrated Russell at the Kennedy Center Honors.


Sources:
Izzo, Jack. "Kurt Russell Didn't Receive Kennedy Center Honor from Trump, despite Claims." Snopes, 2 Apr. 2026, https://www.snopes.com//fact-check/kurt-russell-kennedy-center-trump/.

String theory is uniquely derived from basic assumptions about the universe, physicists show
Story by Whitney Clavin • 4mo • 7 min read


Artwork illustrating how string theory emerges from a few simple mathematical assumptions about particle collisions. Credit: AI-generated art by Clifford Cheung
Artwork illustrating how string theory emerges from a few simple mathematical assumptions about particle collisions. Credit: AI-generated art by Clifford Cheung
If you could take an apple and break it into smaller and smaller parts, you would find molecules, then atoms, followed by subatomic particles like protons and the quarks and gluons that make them up. You might think you hit the bottom, but, according to string theorists, if you keep going to even smaller scales—about a billion billion times smaller than a proton—you will find more: tiny vibrating strings.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Developed in the 1960s, string theory proposes that everything in the universe is made from invisible strings. The theory arose as a possible solution to the problem of "quantum gravity," the quest to align quantum mechanics, which describes our world at the smallest scales, with the general theory of relativity, which explains how our universe works on the largest scales (and includes gravity). Researchers have tried to reconcile the two theories—asking, for example, how gravity behaves in the quantum realm—but their equations go berserk, or in mathematical terms, go to infinity.

String theory is a mathematical solution that tames the unruly infinities. It purports that all particles, including the graviton—the hypothetical particle believed to convey the force of gravity—are generated by very small vibrating strings. The math behind string theory requires the strings to vibrate in at least 10 dimensions, rather than the four we live in (three for space and one for time), which is one of the reasons some scientists are not convinced that string theory is correct. But perhaps the biggest challenge for the theory is the ultrahigh energies required for testing it: Such an experiment would require a particle collider the size of a galaxy.

Related video: Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed
Hello mortals.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
21:10
0
What is a physicist to do? One way they can probe the theory is to turn to a "bootstrap" approach, in which researchers start with certain assumptions they believe to be true about the universe, and then see what laws emerge out of those assumptions. In a new paper titled "Strings from Almost Nothing," accepted for publication in Physical Review Letters, Caltech researchers, and their colleagues at New York University and Institut de Fisica d'Altes Energies in Barcelona, have done just that. From a couple of basic assumptions about how particles should scatter off one another at very high energies, they derived the elements of string theory.

"The strings just fell out," says Clifford Cheung, professor of theoretical physics and director of the Leinweber Forum for Theoretical Physics at Caltech. "We didn't start with any assumptions about strings at all, but then the solution contained the cornerstone signatures of strings."

Though the work does not amount to experimental evidence for string theory, it is "very suggestive from the theoretical viewpoint, since the general assumptions could have yielded infinite solutions, but they yielded only one," Cheung says.

This bootstrap approach helps physicists home in on the defining traits of string theory, explains Hirosi Ooguri, the Fred Kavli Professor of Theoretical Physics and Mathematics at Caltech and the Kent and Joyce Kresa Leadership Chair of the Division of Physics, Mathematics and Astronomy, who is a string theorist though not an author on the paper. "It also helps researchers come up with alternative theories. If string theory is not true, and we want to find another model, then what basic assumptions do we need to remove?" Ooguri says.

Particles in harmony
One of the key signatures of strings that "fell out" of the team's analysis is known as the string spectrum. Discovered by Italian theoretical physicist Gabriele Veneziano of the European Organization for Nuclear Research (CERN) in the late 1960s, the spectrum is an infinite tower, or ladder, of particles, in which the masses and spins increase in discrete steps.

Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Quantum Physics Simplified: From Wave-Particle Duality To Quantum Computing Satisfy Your Curiosity, Explore Field Theory And Other Mind-Bending
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
"At Veneziano's time, particle colliders were seeing this spray of junk come out of the collisions, particles of different masses. It was fascinating and nobody had any idea what was going on. Veneziano wrote down a function to describe all the masses, revealing an infinite tower of particles," Cheung says.

Other researchers later came to realize that Veneziano's tower of particles corresponds to a harmonic series of a vibrating string. If you pluck a violin string, you'll get a series of notes representing the fundamental note and overtones that follow a similar pattern.

String theory was born, but it was not until 1974 that Caltech's John Schwarz, the Harold Brown Professor of Theoretical Physics, Emeritus, and his colleague Joël Scherk, a French physicist, realized that the theory included gravity, thereby forming the first connection between string theory and general relativity.

"Like all particle physicists in that era, we had no prior interest in gravity. String theories are well-behaved at very high energies, unlike Einstein's general theory of relativity, which survives as a low-energy approximation. Therefore, even though much was not yet understood, we were very excited that some version of string theory could provide a unified quantum theory of everything," Schwarz says.

In string theory, different vibrational modes of the tiny strings give rise to the different particles. For instance, a photon arises from an open-ended string vibrating in its fundamental mode while the graviton is thought to result from the fundamental vibrational mode of a closed string.

From the ground up
In the new study, the researchers looked at something called scattering amplitudes, which describe the probabilities of possible outcomes of particle collisions. When researchers formulate scattering amplitudes at higher and higher energies using the tools of general relativity, the unruly infinities crop up. In mathematical terms, this means the results do not make sense and cannot be right.

"If you take general relativity and scatter at very high energies at the so-called Planck scale—that is roughly 19 orders of magnitude greater than a proton's mass—you get a result that makes no sense. Everything completely breaks down," Cheung says.

This is where string theory shines. It prevents the math from going to infinity in a few ways, one of which is called ultrasoftness, whereby the strings soften, or smear out, interactions at extreme high energies, making them more manageable mathematically.

"In a string theory framework, as you increase the energy transfer between particles, you will see a swift falloff in the probability that the particles will scatter. It's like the particles don't even want to scatter off one another, but rather pass freely," Cheung says. "The scattering amplitudes don't go to infinity. It's better behaved."

The researchers took this ultrasoftness trait for particle behavior as one of their starting assumptions. They didn't assume anything about strings but took it to be true that particles have a lower probability of scattering at high energies—something that is needed to tame the unwanted infinities in quantum gravity theories.

In addition, they made another assumption about particle behavior called "minimal zeros," which is more complex. "Remarkably, consistency requires scattering amplitudes not only to interact but also to not interact at special kinematic points called 'zeros.' The assumption of 'minimal zeros' demands the sparsest number of such vanishing points mathematically allowed by the equations," Cheung says.

Starting with the math describing these two assumptions, the researchers rigorously proved that the only mathematical functions that satisfied these assumptions amounted to the defining hallmarks of string theory. These hallmarks include the full spectrum of particle masses and spins as defined by string theory, along with their detailed interaction strengths.

"The precise details of string theory emerged automatically, including the infinite tower of massive spinning particles that form the 'harmonics' of the string that the theory is famous for," says co-author Grant N. Remmen (Ph.D. '17), the James Arthur Postdoctoral Fellow at New York University.

The researchers' bootstrap approach is somewhat like a sudoku puzzle: You start with just a few rules to follow about how to place numbers in a grid and, from these basic rules, search for the one unique solution to the puzzle.

"The deep irony is that this bootstrap idea that we're pursuing now with modern tools and modern ideas is super retro. It's an old idea," Cheung explains. "The original discovery of the Veneziano spectrum, and John Schwarz's work, took a similar approach. They didn't start with string theory models, but rather, the solutions came out of basic principles."

Cheung also points to Caltech's Steven Frautschi as a pioneer of the bootstrap approach. Frautschi, a professor of theoretical physics, emeritus, at Caltech, and his colleague, the late Geoffrey Chew, formerly of UC Berkeley, were the first to develop the bootstrap theory in particle physics in the 1960s (Chew came up with the name after the phrase "pulling oneself up by one's bootstraps"). Frautschi and Chew found early evidence for the infinite tower of particles later discovered by Veneziano.

"The bootstrap idea had become obsolete but now people like Cliff are reviving and modernizing it," Ooguri says. "We now have a better understanding of the basic assumptions we can make, as well as stronger techniques for translating these assumptions into properties of scattering amplitudes and other observables."

More information: Clifford Cheung et al, Strings from almost nothing, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/cw4p-cqh7. On arXiv: DOI: 10.48550/arxiv.2508.09246

Gas pump 'screw method' scam warning is a hoax
Story by Jordan Liles • 4mo • 6 min read


An image shows a photo allegedly showing a screw placed in a gas pump holder by a criminal, superimposed on top of a blurry photo of another gas pump at a Shell gas station. @thatkcguy__ via Instagram and Ronaldo Schemidt via Getty Images, Snopes illustration
An image shows a photo allegedly showing a screw placed in a gas pump holder by a criminal, superimposed on top of a blurry photo of another gas pump at a Shell gas station. @thatkcguy__ via Instagram and Ronaldo Schemidt via Getty Images, Snopes illustration

Claim:

Criminals are inserting screws in gas pump nozzle holders to keep transactions open after customers drive off, allowing the criminals to get free fuel.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

In May 2026, social media users, news media outlets and some law enforcement agencies warned of an alleged "screw method" scam, claiming criminals were inserting screws into gas pump nozzle holders — the housing where nozzles rest when not in use — to defraud consumers. According to the rumor, the screws fool gas pumps into keeping transactions open after customers drive away, allowing criminals to then fill up for free on the previously scanned credit or debit card.

Searches of Bing, DuckDuckGo, Google and Yahoo found no shortage of social media users discussing the gas pump warning, often with specific mentions of a carpenter's screw or drywall screw. Those searches — plus additional reverse image searches — led to posts on Bluesky, Facebook, Instagram (archived), Reddit, TikTok (archived), X and YouTube (archived). Snopes also received reader mail and numerous site searches asking for the truth of this matter.



In short, this was a viral hoax. The people and organizations promoting the unsubstantiated warning failed to cite any credible, verified reports of criminals using screws to defraud consumers at gas pumps. Because of this, we've rated the claim false.

Some news outlets reporting about the warning specifically referenced a May 8 Facebook post (archived) from the Queen Anne's County Office of the Sheriff — located in Maryland — that initially said criminals were carrying out the scam. The office later updated the post to acknowledge its unfounded nature, and a spokesperson told Snopes they had not received credible reports of the scam, saying officers found one report originated as a maintenance issue.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

One of the primary videos (archived) users reposted about the purported scam showed a person looking at screws in gas pump nozzle holders at a Shell station. Shell spokesperson Natalie Gunnell told Snopes, in part, "We are not aware of any reported incidents, described below, occurring at Shell-owned sites. As a standard safeguard, our fuel pumps are designed with an automatic shut-off feature that ends a transaction after a period of inactivity."

25 unexplained astronomical phenomena: Scientists puzzled
Story by Anant Gupta • 2w • 17 min read

25 Unexplained Astronomical Phenomena That Still Puzzle Scientists
The universe is approximately 13.8 billion years old, spanning some 93 billion light-years in diameter — and despite centuries of stargazing, decades of space exploration, and some of the most powerful telescopes ever built, we still don’t understand most of it. Modern astronomy has gifted us with extraordinary insights: we’ve mapped distant galaxies, detected gravitational waves, and photographed black holes. Yet for every question we answer, the cosmos seems to raise a dozen more.

Mathematical Theory &Analysis - Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematics Theory&Application
Mathematical Theory &Analysis - Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematics Theory&Application
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
From signals that appeared once and never returned to invisible forces reshaping the universe itself, the list of genuinely unresolved mysteries in astronomy is both humbling and electrifying. These aren’t fringe curiosities or gaps in fringe science — they’re documented anomalies, reproducible observations, and mathematical contradictions that leave the world’s brightest researchers scratching their heads.

What follows is a journey through 25 unexplained astronomical phenomena that still puzzle scientists today. Some are ancient mysteries hiding in plain sight; others emerged from data collected just a few years ago. All of them represent the frontier where human knowledge ends and the unknown begins.

—

The Cosmic Enigmas: 25 Unexplained Astronomical Phenomena
1. Dark Matter
What it is: Roughly 27% of the universe’s total mass-energy content is made of something that emits no light, reflects no light, and interacts with almost nothing — yet exerts powerful gravitational effects on everything around it.

The Mystery: Galaxies rotate far too fast at their outer edges to be held together by the visible matter they contain. First flagged by Fritz Zwicky in the 1930s and reinforced by Vera Rubin’s galaxy rotation studies in the 1970s, this discrepancy demands something invisible be present. Despite decades of experiments — including deep underground detectors, particle collider searches, and space-based observatories — dark matter has never been directly detected.

Related video: Watch what scientists found beyond our solar system - it wasn't supposed to exist (The Space Race)
View on Watch
View on Watch

The Space Race
Watch what scientists found beyond our solar system - it wasn't supposed to exist
Video Player is loading.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
14:16
0
Current Research: Leading candidates include Weakly Interacting Massive Particles (WIMPs) and axions. None have been confirmed. The mystery deepens because dark matter seems to be fundamental to how galaxies and galaxy clusters form and hold together.

2. Dark Energy
What it is: An even more mysterious component than dark matter, dark energy accounts for roughly 68% of the universe’s total mass-energy density and is thought to drive the accelerating expansion of space itself.

The Mystery: When astronomers discovered in 1998 — using Type Ia supernovae as “standard candles” — that the universe’s expansion is speeding up rather than slowing down, it shattered expectations. Something must be pushing outward with enough force to overcome gravity on cosmic scales. We have no idea what it is. The best description is a “cosmological constant” — a placeholder that admits we’re missing something fundamental about space itself.

AI Ethics: A Textbook (Artificial Intelligence: Foundations, Theory, And Algorithms)
AI Ethics: A Textbook (Artificial Intelligence: Foundations, Theory, And Algorithms)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
3. The Hubble Tension
What it is: The Hubble Constant (H₀) measures how fast the universe is expanding. It should be a single value. Instead, measurements taken in two different ways give two stubbornly different answers.

The Mystery: Data from the early universe — specifically from the Cosmic Microwave Background (CMB) analyzed by the Planck satellite — gives a Hubble Constant of approximately 67.4 km/s/Mpc. But measurements of nearby galaxies and supernovae by projects like SH0ES yield ~73.0 km/s/Mpc. This discrepancy has widened as measurement precision has improved, strongly suggesting it isn’t measurement error. Many physicists now believe it points to new physics — perhaps an unknown particle, a new force, or something stranger entirely.

4. Fast Radio Bursts (FRBs)
What it is: Millisecond-long bursts of radio waves carrying enormous amounts of energy, originating from billions of light-years away. First detected in 2007, hundreds have since been catalogued — and some repeat.

The Mystery: What could produce such intense, short-lived radio pulses across intergalactic distances? Repeating sources like FRB 121102 (located in a dwarf galaxy roughly 3 billion light-years away) and FRB 180916.J0158+65 (which pulses in a 16.35-day cycle) suggest the source isn’t destroyed in the burst. Leading theories include magnetars and compact binary mergers, but no single explanation fits all observed FRBs. With the Canadian Hydrogen Intensity Mapping Experiment (CHIME) now detecting dozens of FRBs per week, the data is multiplying faster than the theories.

5. Tabby’s Star (KIC 8462852)
What it is: A star roughly 1,470 light-years away in the constellation Cygnus, observed by the Kepler space telescope to dim in ways unlike anything ever recorded. Brightness drops of up to 22% were observed, far too large and irregular to be caused by a planet passing in front of it.

The Mystery: Normal transiting exoplanets cause less than 1% dimming and follow predictable patterns. Tabby’s Star dims irregularly, asymmetrically, and for varying durations. Proposed explanations have ranged from massive swarms of cometary fragments to orbiting “megastructures” built by an advanced civilization. The most scientifically favored current hypothesis involves clouds of fine dust, though no model cleanly accounts for all observed behavior. It remains one of astronomy’s weirdest stars.

6. Oumuamua
What it is: Discovered in October 2017, ‘Oumuamua was the first confirmed interstellar object to pass through our solar system. Roughly 100 to 1,000 meters long, it was highly elongated — unlike any known asteroid or comet.

The Mystery: ‘Oumuamua accelerated away from the Sun faster than gravity alone could account for. This “non-gravitational acceleration” is typically explained by outgassing (as in comets), but no outgassing was detected. Its shape, trajectory, and behavior have resisted clean natural explanations. Harvard astronomer Avi Loeb famously proposed it could be an artificial object of extraterrestrial origin — a claim the broader scientific community views skeptically but can’t entirely dismiss because no fully satisfying natural explanation has been confirmed.

7. The Wow! Signal
What it is: On August 15, 1977, astronomer Jerry Ehman was analyzing data from the Big Ear radio telescope at Ohio State University when he noticed a signal so remarkable he circled it in red ink and wrote “Wow!” in the margin. The signal lasted exactly 72 seconds and was detected at 1420.4556 MHz — the frequency of hydrogen, long considered the most likely frequency for interstellar communication.

The Mystery: Despite numerous attempts to re-detect the signal, it has never been repeated. Its characteristics — narrowband frequency, intensity, and directionality — match what a transmission from space would look like. Proposed explanations include a comet’s hydrogen cloud, a classified satellite, or terrestrial interference, but none fully satisfy the data. Nearly five decades later, the Wow! Signal remains the most compelling unidentified radio signal in the history of astronomy.

8. The Great Attractor
What it is: An enormous gravitational anomaly approximately 150 to 250 million light-years away that is drawing our galaxy, our local group, and thousands of other galaxies toward it at speeds of about 600 kilometers per second.

The Mystery: The Great Attractor sits in the “Zone of Avoidance” — a region obscured by the Milky Way’s own gas and dust, making direct observation extremely difficult. Infrared and X-ray telescopes have identified a concentration of galaxies in the region (the Norma Cluster and the broader Laniakea Supercluster), but the full source of gravitational attraction remains poorly mapped. Some evidence points to an even larger structure beyond the Great Attractor: the Shapley Supercluster. What exactly is pulling the observable universe in this direction, and what lies behind our own galaxy’s veil, remains unresolved.

9. The Galactic Center GeV Excess
What it is: The Fermi Gamma-ray Space Telescope detected a spherically symmetric excess of gamma rays emanating from the center of the Milky Way — radiation that shouldn’t be as abundant as it is based on known sources.

The Mystery: Two primary explanations have been proposed: dark matter particles annihilating each other in the dense galactic center (which would be a historic discovery), or an unresolved population of millisecond pulsars too faint for current instruments to detect individually. Both hypotheses fit the data. No definitive evidence for either has emerged. The Galactic Center GeV Excess is a rare astronomical mystery where the competing explanations are both genuinely revolutionary in their own way.

10. The Cold Spot
What it is: A region in the Cosmic Microwave Background (CMB) radiation — the afterglow of the Big Bang — that is significantly colder and larger than standard cosmological models predict. Discovered in 2004 by WMAP and confirmed by the Planck satellite, it sits in the constellation Eridanus and spans roughly 1.8 billion light-years.

The Mystery: Temperature variations in the CMB are normal at small scales, but the Cold Spot is unusually vast and pronounced. Leading explanations include a massive “supervoid” in that direction (a region with far fewer galaxies than average) or — more provocatively — evidence of a collision with a parallel universe during the early moments of cosmic history. The supervoid explanation has some observational support, but it doesn’t fully explain the magnitude of the temperature difference.

11. The Fermi Paradox
What it is: Given the universe’s age (~13.8 billion years), its hundreds of billions of galaxies each containing hundreds of billions of stars, and the growing evidence that planets are extremely common, the statistical probability of intelligent life existing elsewhere seems overwhelming. So where is everybody?

The Mystery: We have detected zero confirmed signs of extraterrestrial intelligence — no signals, no megastructures, no visiting spacecraft. Physicist Enrico Fermi famously noted this contradiction in 1950. Proposed resolutions range from the “Great Filter” (some catastrophic hurdle destroys civilizations before they become spacefaring) to the “Dark Forest” theory (civilizations hide deliberately) to the possibility that we are genuinely alone. No resolution has been proven. The silence of the universe remains one of the most profound unanswered questions in science.

12. The Solar Corona Heating Problem
What it is: The Sun’s surface (the photosphere) burns at approximately 5,800 Kelvin. The layer above it — the corona — reaches temperatures between 1 and 3 million Kelvin. In other words, the atmosphere of the Sun is hundreds of times hotter than its visible surface.

The Mystery: Basic physics says temperature should decrease as you move away from a heat source. The corona violates this rule spectacularly. Two leading explanations exist: “nanoflares” (tiny, rapid magnetic reconnection events adding energy to the corona) and Alfvén wave heating (plasma waves carrying energy outward through the solar atmosphere). Neither explanation has been definitively proven. NASA’s Parker Solar Probe, which has been diving closer to the Sun than any spacecraft before it, is collecting data that may eventually crack this long-standing puzzle.

13. Gamma-Ray Bursts (GRBs)
What it is: The most powerful explosions in the known universe — for a brief moment, a single GRB can outshine an entire galaxy of hundreds of billions of stars. They’re divided into two types: short bursts (less than 2 seconds, likely from neutron star mergers) and long bursts (more than 2 seconds, likely from collapsing massive stars).

The Mystery: While the broad strokes of their origin are increasingly understood, the precise mechanisms behind GRBs — particularly how they achieve such extraordinary energy output and focus it into tight, relativistic jets — remain debated. The “central engine” driving the jets, the role of magnetic fields, and the behavior of the surrounding material all involve physics at extremes we can’t yet fully model. Each new GRB detection adds data that sometimes confirms and sometimes complicates existing theories.

14. Magnetars
What it is: A type of neutron star with a magnetic field so extreme it defies intuition. Magnetars have magnetic fields reaching up to 10¹⁵ Gauss — Earth’s magnetic field is roughly 0.5 Gauss, making magnetars about a quadrillion times more magnetic than our planet.

The Mystery: The mechanism by which neutron stars acquire such extreme magnetism during stellar collapse is not fully understood. How these fields are maintained, why only some neutron stars become magnetars, and how their intense magnetic activity drives periodic bursts of X-rays and gamma rays (including, remarkably, FRBs in at least one known case) remain open questions. Discovered in 1998, magnetars continue to surprise astronomers with behavior that pushes known physics to its limits.

15. Supermassive Black Hole Activity: The Cosmic Volcano
What it is: At the center of a galaxy located millions of light-years away, astronomers observed a supermassive black hole that had been dormant for approximately 100 million years suddenly reactivate — blasting plasma jets nearly a million light-years long into the surrounding space.

The Mystery: What causes a long-dormant black hole to “wake up” so explosively? The trigger mechanism — whether a sudden influx of gas, a merging event, or something else — is not well understood. This kind of event reshapes the surrounding galaxy and the intergalactic medium, but the precise conditions required to flip a black hole from quiescent to violently active remain elusive. Observations like this one highlight how dynamic and unpredictable the universe’s most massive objects truly are.

16. The Black Hole Information Paradox
What it is: When matter falls into a black hole, quantum mechanics insists that the information describing that matter must be preserved somewhere in the universe. But Stephen Hawking’s work showed that black holes eventually evaporate through thermal radiation (Hawking radiation) — and that radiation appears to carry no information about what fell in.

The Mystery: Either quantum mechanics is wrong, general relativity is wrong, or information escapes from black holes in some way we don’t yet understand. This isn’t a philosophical debate — it’s a fundamental contradiction between two of the most successful theories in physics. Proposed resolutions include “black hole complementarity,” “firewalls,” and the holographic principle. After decades of work, a satisfying resolution remains out of reach.

17. Quasar Formation and Evolution
What it is: Quasars — short for “quasi-stellar objects” — are the extraordinarily luminous active nuclei of distant galaxies, powered by supermassive black holes consuming surrounding material. First discovered in 1963, they can outshine their host galaxies by factors of 100 to 1,000.

The Mystery: Some quasars are found at extreme redshifts, meaning they existed when the universe was less than a billion years old. To be that bright that early, their central black holes must have grown to billions of solar masses almost immediately after the Big Bang — far faster than current models of black hole growth allow. How did supermassive black holes form so quickly? And how did early quasars influence galaxy formation in the young universe? These questions are actively investigated by missions including the James Webb Space Telescope.

18. Rogue Planets
What it is: Planets that have been ejected from their original star systems — or possibly formed alone in interstellar space — and now wander the galaxy without a parent star, in permanent darkness.

The Mystery: Estimates suggest there could be billions of rogue planets in the Milky Way alone, potentially outnumbering stars. Some models suggest they could vastly outnumber even that. But how many actually exist, how they’re distributed, and whether their subsurface oceans (kept liquid by internal radioactive heating, not solar warmth) could harbor microbial life are completely open questions. NASA’s upcoming Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope is expected to detect hundreds of rogue planets via gravitational microlensing, which may finally put real numbers on this mysterious population.

19. The Planet Nine Hypothesis
What it is: In 2016, astronomers Konstantin Batygin and Mike Brown proposed the existence of an undetected ninth planet in our outer solar system. The evidence: a statistically improbable clustering of orbits among distant trans-Neptunian objects (TNOs) that seems to require a massive gravitational influence.

The Mystery: If it exists, Planet Nine is estimated to be 5 to 10 times Earth’s mass, orbiting the Sun at hundreds to thousands of astronomical units — far beyond Neptune. Despite targeted searches, it hasn’t been found. Its orbit would take between 10,000 and 20,000 years to complete. Some researchers argue the orbital clustering is a statistical artifact; others believe the planet is real but simply in a region of sky not yet searched thoroughly. The Vera C. Rubin Observatory (LSST), coming online in the mid-2020s, may finally settle the debate.

20. The Origin of Water on Earth
What it is: Earth is the “blue planet” — about 71% of its surface is covered by water. Yet the inner solar system, where Earth formed, was too hot in the early solar system for water ice to condense. Our oceans had to come from somewhere.

The Mystery: Two main delivery mechanisms are proposed: comets and asteroids. Asteroid belt objects, specifically carbonaceous chondrite meteorites, have water with a hydrogen isotope ratio (deuterium-to-hydrogen) that closely matches Earth’s oceans. Some comets match less well. Whether water arrived in a single delivery event, gradually, or through internal outgassing from the planet’s own formation materials is still debated. Missions like the OSIRIS-REx sample return (from asteroid Bennu) are adding data points, but the full story of Earth’s water remains unsettled.

21. The Venus Atmosphere Mystery
What it is: Venus and Earth are nearly identical in size and mass, formed in the same stellar neighborhood, and likely had similar early histories. Yet today, Venus is a hellscape with a surface temperature of 465°C (869°F), a crushing atmospheric pressure 90 times that of Earth, and a thick atmosphere of carbon dioxide with sulfuric acid clouds.

The Mystery: What caused Venus and Earth to diverge so dramatically? Did Venus once have liquid water? If so, what triggered its runaway greenhouse effect? Recent missions, including the European Space Agency’s Venus Express and NASA’s VERITAS (planned), aim to map Venus’s surface and atmosphere in detail. In 2020, astronomers announced the possible detection of phosphine in Venus’s atmosphere — a potential biosignature — though that finding remains highly controversial.

22. Ultra-High-Energy Cosmic Rays
What it is: Cosmic rays are charged particles (mostly protons) that travel through space and occasionally slam into Earth’s atmosphere. Most have relatively modest energies. But a small fraction arrive with energies exceeding 10²⁰ electronvolts — the equivalent of a baseball thrown at about 60 mph packed into a single proton.

The Mystery: No known astrophysical process should be able to accelerate particles to such extreme energies, and at those energies, particles should interact with the CMB and lose energy over long distances (the GZK limit). Yet they’re detected. Their sources — whether active galactic nuclei, gamma-ray bursts, or something else — and the acceleration mechanisms involved are genuinely unknown. The Pierre Auger Observatory in Argentina is the world’s largest cosmic ray detector, but definitive source identification remains elusive.

23. Cosmic Voids
What it is: The universe isn’t uniformly filled with galaxies. It’s structured in a cosmic web of filaments, walls, and clusters — and between them lie vast, nearly empty regions called cosmic voids. The largest known voids span up to 300 million light-years across.

The Mystery: Standard cosmological models predict voids of a certain size and distribution. Some observed voids — including the Boötes Void and the supervoid potentially responsible for the CMB Cold Spot — appear larger or emptier than models comfortably predict. Whether these voids challenge our understanding of cosmic structure formation, dark energy’s role, or something else is an active area of research. They’re also the quietest places in the universe — but what makes them so empty remains partially unexplained.

24. The Lithium Problem
What it is: The Big Bang nucleosynthesis model — which describes how the lightest elements (hydrogen, helium, lithium) were forged in the first few minutes after the Big Bang — makes precise predictions about how much of each element should exist.

The Mystery: While helium abundance matches predictions excellently, the observed amount of primordial lithium-7 in old stars is about three to four times less than the model predicts. This isn’t a small rounding error — it’s a systematic discrepancy seen across many measurements of old, metal-poor stars. Proposed resolutions include unknown nuclear reactions during nucleosynthesis, lithium being absorbed into stars in ways not accounted for, or new physics beyond the Standard Model. After decades, no consensus solution exists.

25. Dark Flow
What it is: In 2008, astrophysicist Alexander Kashlinsky and colleagues proposed that galaxy clusters across vast distances are all moving in the same direction — not toward any visible structure within the observable universe, but toward something beyond its edge.

The Mystery: This “dark flow,” if real, would suggest that the pre-inflationary universe contained enormous structures whose gravity still influences our observable universe from beyond its horizon — a staggering implication that would fundamentally challenge our cosmological picture. Subsequent analyses using Planck data couldn’t confirm the flow, leading many to doubt its existence. But the debate hasn’t fully closed. Whether dark flow is real, a statistical artifact, or a tantalizing hint of something beyond our observable cosmos remains unresolved.

—

Why These Mysteries Matter
These 25 unexplained astronomical phenomena aren’t just intellectually fascinating — they’re scientifically urgent. Dark matter and dark energy together account for 95% of everything in the universe, yet we don’t know what either of them is. The Hubble tension could signal the breakdown of our standard cosmological model. FRBs may reveal new physics of compact objects. Each mystery acts as a lever: solve one, and the implications ripple through multiple fields simultaneously.

The tools to crack some of these puzzles are either already operational or coming online soon. The James Webb Space Telescope is peering further into the early universe than ever before. The Vera C. Rubin Observatory will survey the southern sky with unprecedented depth. The Square Kilometre Array will detect FRBs at a rate that transforms them from curiosities into statistics. Parker Solar Probe continues its daring approach to the Sun. And gravitational wave detectors are listening for events no telescope can see.

The history of astronomy is essentially a history of answers that generated better questions. Solving the mystery of the planets’ orbits led to Newtonian gravity. Understanding stellar spectra revealed the composition of stars. Mapping distant supernovae led to the discovery of dark energy. Today’s 25 unsolved problems are tomorrow’s breakthroughs — provided we keep asking the right questions.

—

FAQ
Q: What is the biggest unsolved mystery in astronomy?

Dark matter and dark energy together represent the most consequential unresolved questions in all of science. Combined, they make up roughly 95% of the universe’s total mass-energy content — yet their fundamental nature remains completely unknown despite decades of intense research.

Q: Has the Wow! Signal ever been explained?

Not definitively. The most frequently cited natural explanation is a hydrogen cloud associated with a comet, but this hypothesis is disputed because the original observations showed no cometary source in that region of sky at the time. The signal has never been detected again, leaving it unexplained after nearly 50 years.

Q: Is Oumuamua really an alien spacecraft?

The mainstream scientific position is that ‘Oumuamua is most likely a natural interstellar object, possibly a fragment of an exoplanet or a type of “nitrogen iceberg.” However, no natural explanation fully accounts for all its observed properties — particularly its non-gravitational acceleration with no detectable outgassing. The alien hypothesis, while considered a fringe view, has been defended seriously by researchers including Harvard’s Avi Loeb.

Q: What is the Hubble tension in simple terms?

Think of it this way: when astronomers measure the universe’s expansion rate using ancient light from the early universe, they get one number. When they measure it using nearby stars and galaxies, they get a different, higher number. These two measurement methods should agree — and they don’t. The discrepancy has grown more pronounced as measurements have become more precise, suggesting something may be fundamentally wrong with our current model of the universe.

Q: Could rogue planets support life?

Theoretically, yes — under very specific conditions. A rogue planet with sufficient mass could retain internal heat from radioactive decay, potentially keeping subsurface oceans liquid even without a star. If such a planet also had a thick hydrogen atmosphere to trap heat, conditions for microbial life might exist. This remains highly speculative, but it’s a scientifically serious hypothesis that astrobiologists are beginning to explore.

Q: How do scientists study phenomena they can’t directly observe, like dark matter?

Primarily through indirect evidence. Dark matter’s gravitational effects bend light (gravitational lensing), influence galaxy rotation speeds, and shape large-scale cosmic structure. Scientists also search for dark matter particles in underground detectors, look for byproducts of dark matter annihilation in space, and attempt to create them in particle accelerators. Building a complete picture from these indirect clues is painstaking — but it’s how much of modern astrophysics works.

—

The Universe Still Has Secrets
The cosmos is not done surprising us. For every galaxy we map, every black hole we image, and every exoplanet we catalog, the universe presents a new layer of complexity that refuses to fit neatly into our existing frameworks. That friction — between what we observe and what we can explain — is precisely where science happens.

These 25 unexplained astronomical phenomena remind us that uncertainty isn’t a failure of science; it’s the engine of it. From the strange silence of the Fermi Paradox to the impossible energies of ultra-high-energy cosmic rays, from the dimming mystery of Tabby’s Star to the universe’s missing lithium, these puzzles are invitations. They invite better instruments, sharper theories, bolder questions, and — eventually — answers that will reshape how humanity understands its place in the cosmos.

The universe keeps its secrets well. But it doesn’t keep them forever.

Last Update: September 1, 2026

Has the Treasury declared the US 'insolvent'? We set the record straight
Story by Jack Izzo & Anna Rascouët-Paz • 5mo • 10 min read


One-dollar, 25-cent, 10-cent and one-cent coins pile up on a U.S. flag. Image courtesy of Jira Hera, accessed via Canva
One-dollar, 25-cent, 10-cent and one-cent coins pile up on a U.S. flag. Image courtesy of Jira Hera, accessed via Canva

Claim:

In March 2026, the U.S. Department of the Treasury declared the U.S. government insolvent.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

Following the U.S. Department of the Treasury's publication of financial documents for the previous fiscal year on March 16, 2026, rumors abounded that it had declared the U.S. government insolvent — meaning the Treasury purportedly announced the government could no longer repay its debts. 

An X user shared the rumor in a March 28, 2026, post (archived), adding that the government had allegedly spent $1.8 trillion more than it had collected in the year ending on Sept. 30, 2025.


Other users shared the claim in posts on Facebook, X and Threads. One post (archived) called the news "major" and lamented that no media outlet was reporting on the alleged development. Several Snopes readers also searched the website and emailed us, seeking to confirm whether the rumor was true. 

The claim stemmed from an opinion piece in Fortune magazine on March 23, 2026. While the original Fortune article was tagged as "commentary" on the publication's website, the piece also appeared on Yahoo Finance. There, the only indication the piece was opinion, not fact, was at the very bottom of the article.

Its author, Steve Hanke, an economist at Johns Hopkins University, asserted that the Treasury had "declared the U.S. insolvent," citing figures from the Treasury's financial report and statements:

That's not hyperbole — it's the conclusion drawn directly from the Treasury Department's own consolidated financial statements for fiscal year 2025, released last week to near-total media silence. The numbers: $6.06 trillion in total assets against $47.78 trillion in total liabilities as of September 30, 2025.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Hanke then asked readers to imagine what the federal budget would look like at the scale of one household:

So consider this: divide every number by 100 million — drop eight zeros — and federal finances look like a household budget in freefall.

That household earns $52,446 and spends $73,378 — running a $20,932 annual deficit. Its total liabilities and unfunded promises amount to $1,361,788 against just $60,554 in assets, leaving it $1.3 million in the hole. Uncle Sam, by any accounting standard, is insolvent.

How Einstein’s final obsession consumed him
Albert Einstein changed science forever with special and general relativity, redefining space, time, and gravity. Yet after his greatest triumphs, he became obsessed with something even bigger — a unified field theory that could tie all of nature’s forces into one elegant framework. For 30 years, he chased this dream, trying to merge gravity, electromagnetism, and quantum mechanics into a single equation. As physics evolved toward the strange new world of quantum theory, Einstein refused to accept its randomness. His lifelong search for hidden order became his final, unreachable horizon.

Why Hungary’s Jewish prodigies outperformed entire nations
Hungary’s disproportionate scientific output can be traced back to its revolutionary education system. Instead of drilling rules, teachers encouraged students to derive formulas themselves, turning classrooms into training grounds for future innovators. Rising anti-Semitism throughout Europe pressured Jewish students to overachieve for safety and opportunity. After World War I, waves of Hungarian intellectuals fled political upheaval, eventually becoming key players in the United States’ scientific rise. From von Neumann to Teller, their contributions shaped everything from modern computers to atomic weapons.

Morning Overview
58.7K Followers
Your mind could be a quantum hologram across space and time
Story by Alexander Clark • 11mo • 3 min read


Your mind could be a quantum hologram across space and time
Your mind could be a quantum hologram across space and time
Scientists are exploring a revolutionary theory that suggests human consciousness might operate as a quantum hologram, potentially extending across space and time. This concept, which integrates principles of quantum mechanics with neural activity, challenges traditional views of the mind. It builds on quantum theories regarding the emergence of space, time, and gravity, as well as insights from naked singularities into the fabric of quantum space-time.

The Holographic Nature of Consciousness
The idea that brain waves might encode consciousness as a holographic projection is gaining traction among researchers. This theory leverages quantum mechanics to explain how the brain achieves perceptual unity despite distributed neural processing. According to a report from Popular Mechanics, brain waves could function as a holographic medium, storing and processing information in a way that mirrors quantum systems. This approach could offer a solution to the “binding problem” in neuroscience, where the brain integrates disparate sensory inputs into a coherent experience.

Quantum effects in biological systems, such as entanglement in microtubules, provide a potential mechanism for holographic information storage in the brain. These microtubules, which are structural components of neurons, might facilitate quantum coherence, allowing for a unified consciousness that transcends the physical boundaries of the brain. This perspective aligns with the idea that consciousness could be a non-local phenomenon, influenced by quantum entanglement.

Quantum Foundations of Space and Time
The hypothesis that space and time emerge from quantum entanglement and informational patterns is a cornerstone of modern theoretical physics. As detailed in a Quanta Magazine article, foundational research in quantum gravity suggests that gravity might be an entropic force arising from holographic boundaries in quantum systems. This perspective challenges classical notions of gravity and offers a new framework for understanding the universe’s fundamental structure.

These theoretical models propose that the arrow of time, or the unidirectional flow of events, is linked to quantum fluctuations. This connection between quantum mechanics and the macroscopic reality we experience daily provides a compelling explanation for the emergence of time as we perceive it. By viewing space and time as emergent properties of quantum systems, scientists are redefining our understanding of reality itself.

Naked Singularities in Quantum Space-Time
Naked singularities, which are singularities not cloaked by event horizons, offer unique insights into the quantum structure of space-time. According to a New Scientist article, these phenomena reveal the breakdown of classical general relativity and the emergence of quantum corrections. By studying naked singularities, researchers can explore how higher-dimensional information projects onto lower-dimensional surfaces, a concept known as holographic duality.

This exploration of naked singularities provides a window into the fundamental nature of the universe, challenging established theories and opening new avenues for understanding quantum space-time. The revelations from these singularities could have profound implications for our comprehension of the cosmos, potentially altering our perception of reality and the laws that govern it.

Implications for Consciousness Across Dimensions
The quantum hologram model of consciousness suggests that mental states could transcend physical locality through entanglement. This idea, as discussed in the Popular Mechanics article, posits that consciousness might be capable of linking distant events, offering a new perspective on the nature of awareness. Experimental approaches, such as testing quantum coherence in brain tissue, could provide evidence for this model, potentially validating the hypothesis that consciousness operates on a quantum level.

By integrating these quantum insights, scientists are paving the way for a new era of exploration into the mysteries of consciousness and the universe.

Trump pledged $5K 'dividend' to every US adult if GOP wins midterms. Can he do that?
Story by Rae Deng • 1w • 4 min read


U.S. President Donald Trump speaks to a large crowd waving American flags. . Andrew Harnick, accessed via Getty Images
U.S. President Donald Trump speaks to a large crowd waving American flags. . Andrew Harnick, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

U.S. President Donald Trump has promised $5,000 to every American adult if the Republicans win majority control of the Senate and House of Representatives in the 2026 midterm elections.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

Context:

Trump has floated similar ideas before that have not led anywhere as of this writing. Furthermore, the president cannot unilaterally give Americans government money without congressional approval, nor is it clear how he would pay the approximately $1.2 trillion cost.

 
As the 2026 midterm election season kicked off, social media users claimed U.S. President Donald Trump said he would give every American adult $5,000 if Republicans win majority control of the government. 

The claim spread on X and Reddit. Many of the posts included clips of Trump appearing to make such a promise. Other posts suggested that such a promise would amount to bribery. 

Trump did, in fact, make this pledge during the Sept. 9 Republican National Committee midterm convention in Dallas, Texas, with the added condition that Americans would have to spend the money in the United States. As such, we have rated this claim as true. 

However, such a proposal would require congressional approval and would cost approximately $1.2 trillion, making it an unrealistic promise. 

Shortly after Trump's comments, Vice President JD Vance suggested in a Fox News interview that U.S. tariff revenue could pay for the so-called "Trump dividend" (see 5:50). However, credible estimates suggest the United States hasn't made anywhere near enough through tariffs to cover such a dividend.  

While Congress signed off on one-time cash payments to Americans under the first Trump administration amid the COVID-19 pandemic, the president has floated similar ideas during his second term that have not materialized as of this writing. For example, he said he "loved" the idea of paying Americans a percentage of any money saved by the short-lived Department of Government Efficiency, or DOGE. The president also said on Truth Social in November 2025 that he would pay a "dividend of at least $2,000" to "everyone." 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Multiple readers asked us if the "Trump dividend" counted as bribing people to vote, or paying for votes, which is illegal under federal law. Vance defended the dividend idea against such allegations during his Fox News interview (see 5:28).

"The president is saying, if you keep it going, to the American people — if you keep us in power and allow us to continue to do these things, then you will share in some of the benefit of this incredible wealth that we are creating in the United States of America," he said at 6:25.

8mo8 months ago, 12/31/2025, 3:56 AM
What Feynman showed about motion changes how reality works
Classical physics suggests that objects move along a single, well-defined path. Quantum mechanics says something far stranger: particles explore every possible path simultaneously. According to Feynman’s formulation, all paths contribute, but most cancel each other out through interference. Only the paths near the one of least action survive, creating the illusion of a single trajectory. What appears orderly and predictable is actually the result of massive underlying complexity cancelling itself away.

They found a deadly skyscraper mistake - and kept it secret from New York
In 1978, engineers discovered that New York’s iconic Citicorp Center skyscraper had a catastrophic structural flaw that could cause the entire tower to collapse during powerful winds. With hurricane season approaching, crews secretly entered the building every night to weld emergency steel plates onto hundreds of critical joints while thousands of office workers continued their jobs above them — completely unaware of the danger. City officials even prepared a massive evacuation plan in case the tower began to fail, fearing a chain-reaction collapse across Manhattan. This video reveals the unbelievable true story of how one hidden engineering mistake nearly caused one of the worst urban disasters in American history.

Chemists overturn 40-year assumption about a key class of superconductors
Story by Science X staff • 1d • 3 min read


(a) Representation of the LTO (left) and LTT (right) structures, with A-site cation omitted for clarity. Oxygens are shown in teal and magenta, indicating whether they are above and below the CuO2 planes, respectively. In LTO, the sense of the distortion propagates along [010], with all layers aligned. In LTT, the sense of the distortion propagates along alternating diagonals, with a 90° rotation, from [110] to [1 ¯ 1 0], between adjacent layers. (b) 300 K reconstruction of the sample from all reflections and filtered by A- and B-centered systematic absences, revealing twin domains. (c) Order parameter map indicating X+3 propagation direction with black as LTO and teal as LTT. (d) 300 K strain reconstruction based on Γ+4 showing strong correlations with the systematic absence map. Axes are given with respect to the LTO and LTT basis. Credit: Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/r62x-kt5j
(a) Representation of the LTO (left) and LTT (right) structures, with A-site cation omitted for clarity. Oxygens are shown in teal and magenta, indicating whether they are above and below the CuO2 planes, respectively. In LTO, the sense of the distortion propagates along [010], with all layers aligned. In LTT, the sense of the distortion propagates along alternating diagonals, with a 90° rotation, from [110] to [1 ¯ 1 0], between adjacent layers. (b) 300 K reconstruction of the sample from all reflections and filtered by A- and B-centered systematic absences, revealing twin domains. (c) Order parameter map indicating X+3 propagation direction with black as LTO and teal as LTT. (d) 300 K strain reconstruction based on Γ+4 showing strong correlations with the systematic absence map. Axes are given with respect to the LTO and LTT basis. Credit: Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/r62x-kt5j
Scientists at Warwick have shown that a material treated for 40 years as a uniform, textbook superconductor is in fact a patchwork of different crystal structures throughout its bulk, using an advanced 3D imaging technique to see deep inside the crystal for the first time.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

An Introduction To Topological Groups (London Mathematical Society Lecture Note Series, Series Number 15) (Volume 0)
An Introduction To Topological Groups (London Mathematical Society Lecture Note Series, Series Number 15) (Volume 0)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
High-temperature superconductivity is widely regarded as one of the most significant scientific discoveries of the past 40 years. It promises technologies built on electricity that flows with zero resistance and magnets far more powerful than anything possible today, and it underpins research into future power grids, medical scanners and quantum computers.

The study, published in Physical Review Letters and led by researchers in the Department of Chemistry at the University of Warwick working with the European Synchrotron Radiation Facility (ESRF) in France, looked inside a type of superconductor called a cuprate, a copper-based material that can carry electricity with no resistance at unusually high, though still very cold, temperatures.

A patchwork inside
For 40 years, most theories about how cuprates work have assumed their crystal structure is the same all the way through. Using a technique called scanning 3D X-ray diffraction, which works a bit like a medical CT scan but for the arrangement of atoms inside a crystal, the team was able to build a three-dimensional picture of the material's interior for the first time.

Related video: Experiments whose results seemed to break physics until researchers dug deeper (list25)
View on Watch
View on Watch

list25
Experiments whose results seemed to break physics until researchers dug deeper
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
22:32
0
What they found was a crystal divided into regions with two subtly different atomic arrangements, separated by boundaries hundreds of times wider than anyone expected to see between two crystal structures. These boundaries were so wide that they behaved almost like a structure in their own right, rather than a simple dividing line.

Professor Mark Senn, of the University of Warwick's Department of Chemistry, who led the study, said, "For 40 years, the working assumption has been that these materials are the same all the way through, and nearly all the theory is built on that picture. We've shown it doesn't hold. The crystal is fundamentally patchworked and textured, with unusually wide boundary regions that likely work against superconductivity rather than just sitting alongside it.

"This might explain why some materials perform better than others and means some existing bulk measurements will need to be reinterpreted and future theoretical models built that incorporate this structural complexity."

Mathematical Theory &Analysis - Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematics Theory&Application
Mathematical Theory &Analysis - Advances in Pure Mathematics - Mathematics Theory&Application
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The team believes this kind of hidden structure is likely common across the wider family of cuprate superconductors, and possibly in other related materials being explored for superconductivity under extreme pressure. They also see the method itself, enabled by a recent 150 million-euro upgrade to the ESRF, as a breakthrough: It opens up the ability to look inside a wide range of materials in 3D, at a level of detail not previously possible, to understand how their internal structure shapes the way they behave.

More information: Evie Ladbrook et al, Giant Domain Walls and Intrinsic Heterogeneity in 214 Cuprate Superconductors, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/r62x-kt5j

Provided by University of Warwick

Did Kurt Russell say, 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents'?
Story by Jordan Liles • 1mo • 2 min read


An image shows a close-up of actor Kurt Russell wearing a black bow tie, black suit, white dress shirt and tinted sunglasses at a red carpet-style event. Valery Hache accessed via Getty Images
An image shows a close-up of actor Kurt Russell wearing a black bow tie, black suit, white dress shirt and tinted sunglasses at a red carpet-style event. Valery Hache accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

Actor Kurt Russell once said, "Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents."

Rating:

Incorrect Attribution (About this rating?)

In July 2026, people claimed film and TV actor Kurt Russell once said, "Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents."

For example, on July 24, David J. Harris Jr., who hosts the Newsmax TV show "The Pulse," posted on Facebook (archived) a photo of Russell that displayed the word "BREAKING" with two police car light emojis. The post's text read, "BREAKING: Famous actor, Kurt Russell, came out STRONG against woke parents. He says: 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents.'" Harris made the same exact post (archived) on the previous day.


Did Kurt Russell say, 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents'?
Did Kurt Russell say, 'Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents'?
(David J. Harris Jr. accessed via Facebook)

Other users shared the same alleged quote on Facebook (archived), Instagram (archived) and X (archived).

In short, Russell did not say this. Users shared a fabricated quote without presenting evidence of Russell ever making the remark.

Snopes reached out by email to a Russell representatives with United Talent. We asked for official confirmation that Russell never said the alleged quote and will update this article if we receive that confirmation.

Researching the quote
Searches of DuckDuckGo and Google failed to locate any news media outlets reporting Russell made the remark, "Children don't need new genders, they need normal parents." Entertainment and political outlets would have widely reported on the politically charged matter had he truly made the comment.

Additional searches for the quote on the online book archive Google Books, newspaper archive Newspapers.com and YouTube found no further details, either.

Those searches, as well as reverse image searches, located several memes containing the quote on Facebook (archived) Instagram (archived), Threads (archived) and X (archived). According to the searches, the earliest posts attributing the quote to Russell dated back to at least May 2026. We have not yet confirmed the original source of the quote.

Months earlier in March, users shared a similar rumor falsely alleging people boycotted Russell after he said children should not be allowed to watch cartoons with LGBTQ+ themes. No evidence supported the claim Russell made that remark, either.

For further reading, we previously reported the truth of whether U.S. President Donald Trump celebrated Russell at the Kennedy Center Honors.


Sources:
Izzo, Jack. "Kurt Russell Didn't Receive Kennedy Center Honor from Trump, despite Claims." Snopes, 2 Apr. 2026, https://www.snopes.com//fact-check/kurt-russell-kennedy-center-trump/.

A thermodynamic approach to gravity could explain cosmic acceleration without dark energy
Story by Ingrid Fadelli • 2mo • 4 min read


The thermodynamic cycle proposed in the Letter. Standard Einstein gravity corresponds to the degenerate case in which only heat-flow legs are present. Allowing the additional work-producing legs opens the door to new gravitational theories, including ones in which matter-energy conservation is modified. Credit: Isichei and Magueijo / Physical Review Letters.
The thermodynamic cycle proposed in the Letter. Standard Einstein gravity corresponds to the degenerate case in which only heat-flow legs are present. Allowing the additional work-producing legs opens the door to new gravitational theories, including ones in which matter-energy conservation is modified. Credit: Isichei and Magueijo / Physical Review Letters.
Gravity, the force that attracts objects toward each other, is currently framed by Albert Einstein's theory of general relativity. This framework describes gravity as the curvature of spacetime, the invisible four-dimensional fabric of the universe.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

An Introduction To Topological Groups (London Mathematical Society Lecture Note Series, Series Number 15) (Volume 0)
An Introduction To Topological Groups (London Mathematical Society Lecture Note Series, Series Number 15) (Volume 0)
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
While general relativity is now the central theory of gravity, it fails to explain some cosmological phenomena and mysteries, such as the so-called cosmological constant problem. This is the unexplained mismatch between the observed energy of empty space and the far greater values predicted by quantum theories.

In a recent paper published in Physical Review Letters, researchers at Imperial College London tried to frame gravity using thermodynamics, the framework that describes how energy and heat transform. Their study builds on a seminal paper by theoretical physicist Ted Jacobson, published more than three decades ago.

"I first came across Jacobson's seminal 1995 work when I was just out of my Ph.D., and I found the idea fascinating," João Magueijo, senior author of the paper, told Phys.org.

"He inverted the logic of Hawking and Bekenstein's arguments that Einstein gravity has temperature and entropy and instead used thermal physics to derive Einstein gravity. I wanted to do something with this idea for years, but all my attempts failed miserably. Then last year, while on holiday on a remote Greek island, part of which has no internet, which may have helped, I realized that most previous work had tried to retrofit existing theories of gravity into Jacobson's construction."


A small causal diamond used as a tiny local laboratory for deriving gravity from thermal physics. Heat flows in and out across the light-like boundaries of the diamond, allowing the authors to ask what kind of gravitational theory emerges from a more general thermodynamic process that might happen inside the diamond. Credit: Isichei and Magueijo / Physical Review Letters.
A small causal diamond used as a tiny local laboratory for deriving gravity from thermal physics. Heat flows in and out across the light-like boundaries of the diamond, allowing the authors to ask what kind of gravitational theory emerges from a more general thermodynamic process that might happen inside the diamond. Credit: Isichei and Magueijo / Physical Review Letters.
Building on this realization, Magueijo started exploring the possibility of describing gravity starting from thermal physics alone, without trying to determine what type of gravity theory would emerge. His hope was that this process would lead to entirely new theories of gravity that no one had thought of before.

Related video: Why black holes aren't actually holes (XPLNR)
View on Watch
View on Watch

XPLNR
Why black holes aren't actually holes
A black hole isn't actually a hole at all.
Current Time 
0:01
/
Duration 
0:51
0
Linking gravity, thermodynamics and the expanding universe
To further develop the ideas he had been contemplating, Magueijo started collaborating with Ray Isichei, a Ph.D. student he was supervising at Imperial College. Together, the two researchers started examining gravity from a thermodynamic standpoint, specifically framing it as an Otto cycle, a thermodynamic construct that describes how gasoline engines work.

"We asked what happens if the thermodynamic process behind gravity is not just heat flow," Magueijo explained. "In ordinary thermodynamics, heat is almost never the whole story: There may also be chemical reactions, expansion against a piston, work being done or other contributions. So, we added this missing 'something else' to the argument, without prejudice regarding what would come out the other side."

To their surprise, the researchers found that the gravitational theory they derived allowed matter and energy to be created or destroyed. This was a total shock, as the conservation of energy and matter is a fundamental physical principle. The fact that it could be violated almost prompted them to abandon their theory altogether.

Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Team of Bestsellers
Ghostwriting & Publishing - Writers With 30+ Years Of Exp. - Team of Bestsellers
nexusbookwriting.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
"The idea did not end up in the garbage bin because we realized that, when applied to the universe as a whole, it could reproduce the observed acceleration of cosmic expansion without having to posit dark energy, a cosmological constant, or any of the usual ingredients invoked to explain it," Magueijo said.

"Normal matter should pull back and decelerate the expansion of the universe, but that assumes the usual conservation laws. In this model, normal matter whose conservation law is modified (allowing for continuous creation) can instead drive acceleration."

Fueling new theoretical studies
The team's study offers a fresh and unconventional theory of gravity, suggesting that Einstein's theory of relativity could also potentially be framed as a thermodynamic process. This theoretical framework could eliminate the need for a conventional cosmological constant, potentially helping to tackle a long-standing issue in cosmology.

While the new theory devised by Magueijo and Isichei is intriguing, it is still speculative and in its early stages. The researchers are now planning further studies aimed at developing it further and comparing its predictions with available cosmological evidence and experimental results.

"A lot of work now needs to be done comparing the model in detail with cosmological observations," Magueijo added. "When I started my Ph.D., back in 1990, you could still say almost anything in cosmology, because the paucity of data allowed it. Cosmology has since become a high-precision, data-driven subject. Any new idea now must pass the gauntlet of observation."

Written for you by our author Ingrid Fadelli,edited by Gaby Clark, and fact-checked and reviewed by Robert Egan—thisarticle is the result of careful human work. We rely on readers like you to keep independent science journalism alive.If this reporting matters to you,please consider a donation (especially monthly).

More information: Raymond Isichei et al, Lorentz Violation in Emergent Gravity and Its Cosmological Consequences, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/tvmx-qk3k.

Have Social Security checks been renamed 'federal benefit payments'? Here's the bottom line
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 5mo • 4 min read


Image shows a hand holding a cell phone that reveals the Social Security Administration logo. Marcin Golba, accessed via Getty Images
Image shows a hand holding a cell phone that reveals the Social Security Administration logo. Marcin Golba, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

Social Security checks have officially been renamed "federal benefit payments."

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

In late March 2026, long-running internet rumors claimed U.S. Social Security payments were being renamed "federal benefit payments." The rumor had spread in lengthy Facebook posts for several years, describing the "rebrand" as undermining "the moral and financial contract between workers and the government."

One popular variant claimed the government was taking money from people's earned income and saying it would be used as a "benefit" payment when people were entitled to that money. Here is an excerpt:  

I am forwarding it because it touches a nerve in me, and I hope it will in you. Please keep passing it on until everyone in our country has read it. The government is now referring to our Social Security checks as a "Federal Benefit Payment." This isn't a benefit. It is our money paid out of our earned income! Not only did we all contribute to Social Security but our employers did too. It totaled 15% of our income before taxes.

If you averaged $30K per year over your working life, that's close to $180,000 invested in Social Security. If you calculate the future value of your monthly investment in social security ($375/month, including both you and your employers contributions) at a meager 1% interest rate compounded monthly, after 40 years of working you'd have more than $1.3+ million dollars saved! This is your personal investment. Upon retirement, if you took out only 3% per year, you'd receive $39,318 per year, or $3,277 per month. 

That's almost three times more than today's average Social Security benefit of $1,230 per month, according to the Social Security Administration. (Google it – it's a fact).

Expand article logo  Continue reading

And your retirement fund would last more than 33 years (until you're 98 if you retire at age 65)! I can only imagine how much better most average-income people could live in retirement if our government had just invested our money in low-risk interest-earning accounts.

Instead, the folks in Washington pulled off a bigger "Ponzi scheme" than Bernie Madoff ever did. They took our money and used it elsewhere. They forgot (oh yes, they knew) that it was OUR money they were taking. They didn't have a referendum to ask us if we wanted to lend the money to them. And they didn't pay interest on the debt they assumed. And recently they've told us that the money won't support us for very much longer.

But is it our fault they misused our investments? And now, to add insult to injury, they're calling it a "benefit", as if we never worked to earn every penny of it. Just because they borrowed the money doesn't mean that our investments were a charity!

 


Leadgen logo

Scientists cannot explain weak gravity unless another dimension exists
Gravity is dramatically weaker than the universe’s other fundamental forces, and physicists still do not fully understand why. One theory proposes that our visible universe is trapped inside a membrane surrounded by additional spatial dimensions. While other forces remain confined within our universe, gravity may leak into the higher-dimensional space beyond it. If the theory is correct, other universes could exist incredibly close to ours while remaining completely impossible for us to perceive.
